On certain invariants of compact quantum groups
Jacek Krajczok, Piotr M. Sołtan

Abstract
. We introduce and study a number of invariants of locally compact quantum groups defined by their scaling and modular groups and the spectrum of their modular elements. Focusing mainly on compact quantum groups we consider the question whether triviality of one of the invariants is equivalent to the quantum group being of Kac type and show that it has a positive answer in many cases including duals of second countable type I \mathrm{I} discrete quantum groups. We perform a complete calculation of the invariants for all q q -deformations of compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie groups as well as for some non-compact quantum groups and the compact quantum groups U F + \operatorname{U}_{F}^{+} . Finally we introduce a family of conditions for discrete quantum groups which for classical discrete groups are all equivalent to the fact that the group is i.c.c. We show that the above mentioned question about characterization of Kac type quantum groups by one of our invariants has a positive answer for duals of discrete quantum groups satisfying such an i.c.c.-type condition and illustrate this with the example of U F + \operatorname{U}_{F}^{+} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
46L67, 20G42

1.
Introduction

In this paper we investigate certain invariants of compact and locally compact quantum groups which grew out of our previous investigations into compact quantum groups
\mathbb{G}
with
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
a factor (
[
23
]
). In that paper we constructed various families of compact quantum groups and the techniques used to show that members of these families are pairwise non-isomorphic included looking at three invariants in the form of subgroups of
\mathbb{R}
assigned to a locally compact quantum group. They are all similar to the famous Connes invariant
T
for von Neumann algebras, but the modular group is replaced by the scaling group of the quantum group.

In this paper we take a much more systematic approach to these invariants. First of all we extend the family of invariants to seven items (see detailed discussion in Section
2
) for
\mathbb{G}
and seven for its Pontriagin dual
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. The additional invariants are related to the modular group of the Haar measure (in fact one of our invariants is exactly
T(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))
) and the spectrum of the modular element.

The leitmotif of the paper is the statement and study of a conjecture relating triviality of one of the invariants for a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
with the question whether
\mathbb{G}
is of Kac type (Conjecture
1
). Although at first sight the conjecture does not likely to hold in much generality, we have been able to prove it in several cases. This includes the case of second countable compact quantum groups with
2
-dimensional representation satisfying
\dim{U}<\qdim{U}
(Section
3.1
) and duals of second countable discrete quantum groups of type
\mathrm{I}
(Section
3.2
). Furthermore, we discuss a family of conditions which we call i.c.c.-type conditions which for duals of classical discrete groups are all equivalent to the group being i.c.c., and turn out to imply the validity of our main conjecture.

Sections
4
and
5
are devoted to computation of the values of our invariants. In Section
4
we develop a rather extensive machinery in order to compute all the invariants for
q
-deformations
G_{q}
of all compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie groups. Along the way we encounter an interesting phenomenon that there are compact quantum groups (e.g.
\operatorname{SU}_{q}(3)
) with non-trivial inner scaling automorphisms which are not implemented by a group-like unitary. In fact the implementing unitaries do not even belong to
\C(G_{q})
(see Section
4.4
). Next, in Section
5
, we include computations of the invariants for two families of non-compact quantum groups (the quantum deformations of the
\operatorname{E}(2)
and the “
az+b
” groups) and for the compact quantum group
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
. This compact quantum group also serves as an illustration of the results about i.c.c. type conditions mentioned above (see Section
6.1
).

Throughout the paper we have adhered to the standard conventions of the theory of compact and locally compact quantum groups. In particular, given a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
the symbol
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
denotes the associated von Neumann algebra acting on the GNS Hilbert space
\Ltwo(\mathbb{G})
of the left Haar measure and
\Lone(\mathbb{G})
stands for the predual of
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
. The reduced
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra of functions on
\mathbb{G}
is denoted by
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
or by
\C(\mathbb{G})
(if
\mathbb{G}
is compact) or
\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
(if
\mathbb{G}
is discrete). A locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is called
second countable
if either of the following conditions holds:

•

\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is separable,

•

\Lone(\mathbb{G})
is separable,

•

\Ltwo(\mathbb{G})
is separable

(
[
20
, Lemma 14.6]
). We say that a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is
type
\mathrm{I}
if
\C_{0}^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is a type
\mathrm{I}

\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra (also see
[
20
]
). For the general theory of locally compact quantum groups we refer the reader to
[
26
,
27
,
43
]
as well as the books
[
38
,
39
]
, while for compact quantum groups we recommend the book
[
28
]
.

The theory of locally compact quantum groups uses many standard notions from the theory of operator algebras and the Tomita-Takesaki theory, for which the reader is advised to consult
[
11
,
12
,
32
,
37
]
and
[
8
]
as well as any of the many other monographs on the subject. In particular, if
\theta
is a normal semifinite faithful (n.s.f.) weight on a von Neumann algebra
\mathrm{M}
then
\mathfrak{N}_{\theta}=\bigl\{x\in\mathrm{M}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\theta(x^{*}x)<+\infty\bigr\}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\theta}=\operatorname{span}\mathfrak{N}_{\theta}^{*}\mathfrak{N}_{\theta}
. The GNS map for
\theta
will be denoted by
\Lambda_{\theta}
.

We will also use some of the less standard notation, namely for a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
we will write
\Linf(\mathbb{G})^{\sigma}
for the elements of the von Neumann algebra
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
invariant under the modular group of the Haar measure of
\mathbb{G}
, which itself will be denoted by
{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}
(i.e.
\Linf(\mathbb{G})^{\sigma}
is the
centralizer
of
{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}
). The canonical cyclic vector for the GNS representation of
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
associated to
{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}
will be denoted by
\Omega{G}
.

In order to stay consistent with the notation of
[
21
,
20
]
and
[
26
]
, a representation
U
of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
on a (in all cases finite dimensional) Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{U}
is a unitary element of
\C(\mathbb{G})\otimes\B(\mathscr{H}_{U})
satisfying
(\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(U)=U_{13}U_{23}
.

The dimension of
U
will be denoted by
\dim{U}
and the quantum dimension (
[
28
, Section 1.4]
) by
\qdim{U}
. Note that if
\alpha
denotes the equivalence class of
U
then the notions of dimension and quantum dimension of
\alpha
make perfect sense and we will often use symbols like
\dim{\alpha}
or
\qdim{\alpha}
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact quantum group and let
\Irr{\mathbb{G}}
denote the set of equivalence classes of all irreducible representations of
\mathbb{G}
. We will often choose unitary representatives
U^{\alpha}\in\alpha
(in fact we will only consider unitary representations) and then choose bases in the corresponding Hilbert spaces
\{\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Irr{\mathbb{G}}}
in which the operators
\uprho_{\alpha}
are diagonal with eigenvalues
\uprho_{\alpha,1},\dotsc,\uprho_{\alpha,\dim{\alpha}}
(sometimes we will also ask that these eigenvalues are in increasing order). The corresponding matrix elements
U^{\alpha}_{i,j}
of the representations
U^{\alpha}
transform under the modular and scaling groups of
\mathbb{G}
as follows:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \sigma^{\boldsymbol{h}}_{t}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})&=\uprho_{\alpha,i}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\uprho_{\alpha,j}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\\
\tau{G}_{t}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})&=\uprho_{\alpha,i}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\uprho_{\alpha,j}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\end{aligned}\right.,\qquad{t}\in\mathbb{R},\>i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,\dim{\alpha}\}.

Finally let us mention that in Sections
3.2
and
4.1
we will use a very convenient notation for eigenspaces and eigenprojections of self-adjoint (and other) operators. Namely if
\mathscr{H}
is a Hilbert space and
T
is a self-adjoint operator on
\mathscr{H}
(not necessarily bounded) then the subspace
\ker(\lambda\mathds{1}-T)
will be denoted by
\mathscr{H}(T=\lambda)
, while the orthogonal projection onto
\mathscr{H}(T=\lambda)
will be written as
\chi(T=\lambda)
. Similarly, given an interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
the projection
\chi_{[a,b]}(T)
will be denoted by the symbol
\chi(a\leq{T}\leq{b})
and its range by
\mathscr{H}(a\leq{T}\leq{b})
. We will also use an obvious extension of this notation to any normal operator
N
and any measurable subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
according to which
\chi_{\Omega}(N)
is denoted
\chi(N\in\Omega)
and the range of this projection is
\mathscr{H}(N\in\Omega)
.

2.
The invariants and some of their properties

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group. In
[
23
]
we introduced the following invariants of
\mathbb{G}
, related to the behavior of the scaling group:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\Ttau(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\tau_{t}{G}=\mathrm{id}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\tau_{t}{G}\in\operatorname{Inn}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))\bigr\},

\displaystyle\TtauAInn(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\tau_{t}{G}\in\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))\bigr\},

where
\operatorname{Inn}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))
and
\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))
are respectively the group of inner automorphisms and the group of approximately inner automorphisms of the von Neumann algebra
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
.

Some basic properties and examples of calculation of these invariants were recorded in
[
23
, Section 5]
, in particular for any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
equality
\Ttau(\mathbb{G})=\Ttau(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
holds.

In addition to the invariants (
2.1
) we will study invariants related to modular automorphisms and the modular element.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group with left Haar measure
\varphi
and modular element
\delta
. We define the following subsets of
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\Tsig(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}=\mathrm{id}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\in\operatorname{Inn}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))\bigr\},

\displaystyle\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}\in\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))\bigr\},

\displaystyle\Mod(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\delta^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=\mathds{1}\bigr\}.

It is sometimes convenient to use the notation
T^{\circ}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{G})
allowing
\circ
and
\bullet
to stand for
\tau
or
\sigma
, and
\operatorname{Inn}
,
\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}
or nothing respectively. We will indicate it by considering
T^{\circ}_{\bullet}
for
\circ\in\{\tau,\sigma\}
and, rather unconventionally,
\bullet\in\bigl\{\ \,,\operatorname{Inn},\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}\bigr\}
.

Remark 2.2
.

(1)

The sets introduced in the Definition
2.1
are subgroups of
\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore,
\Tsig(\mathbb{G})
,
\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})
, and
\Mod(\mathbb{G})
are closed. Indeed, for
\Tsig(\mathbb{G})
and
\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})
the argument is analogous to
[
23
, Proposition 5.3]
, whereas closedness of
\Mod(\mathbb{G})
follows from the spectral theorem for self-adjoint operators (cf. e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 10.5]
).

(2)

We would obtain the same groups
\Tsig(\mathbb{G})
,
\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})
, and
\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})
if we chose the right Haar measure instead of the left one – this is a consequence of the equalities

\nabla_{\!\psi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\nabla_{\!\varphi}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}J_{\widehat{\varphi}},\quad\sigma_{t}^{\psi}(x)=\delta^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(x)\delta^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}

valid for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and any
x\in\Linf(\mathbb{G})
.

(3)

\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})
is equal to the Connes’ invariant
T(\Linf(\mathbb{G}))
. Consequently,
\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})
depends only on the von Neumann algebra
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
. It is also the case for
\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})
(see
[
37
, Theorem VIII.3.3]
).

(4)

If
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
are isomorphic locally compact quantum groups, then
T^{\circ}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{G})=T^{\circ}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{H})
for all values of
\circ
and
\bullet
considered above and similarly
\Mod(\mathbb{G})=\Mod(\mathbb{H})
.

We will now gather some general properties of the above invariants.

Proposition 2.3
.

For any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
we have

(2.2a)

\displaystyle\Tsig(\mathbb{G})

\displaystyle=\Ttau(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),

(2.2b)

\displaystyle\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle=\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),

(2.2c)

\displaystyle\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle=\TtauAInn(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),

(2.2d)

\displaystyle\Mod(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})

\displaystyle\subset\tfrac{1}{2}\Ttau(\mathbb{G}).

Equation (
2.2a
) can be seen as a “local version” of
[
21
, Lemma 6.2]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

Recall that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\nabla_{\!\psi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=\widehat{\delta}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}P^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}
(where
P
is defined in
[
26
, Definition 6.9]
), thus if
t\in\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
then
\nabla_{\!\psi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=P^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}
and consequently
\sigma^{\psi}_{t}=\tau{G}_{-t}
. From this we deduce (
2.2b
), (
2.2c
) and containment
\supseteq
in (
2.2a
).

Inclusion
\Tsig(\mathbb{G})\subset\Ttau(\mathbb{G})\cap\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
can be shown in the same way as in
[
21
, Lemma 6.2]
, let us recall this reasoning. Take
t\in\Tsig(\mathbb{G})
. Then we have
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}={\widehat{\delta}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}}\in\Linf(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Since
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
commutes with
J_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, we have
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\in\cZ(\Linf(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
and consequently
\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{t}=\mathrm{id}
, i.e.
t\in\Ttau(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. As
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
implements
\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{t}
in the sense that
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(x)=\widehat{\nu}^{\scriptstyle{t\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{t}(x))=\widehat{\nu}^{\scriptstyle{t\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(x)
for
x
square integrable for
\widehat{\varphi}
, we conclude
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=\widehat{\nu}^{\scriptstyle{t\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}\mathds{1}
and taking norm of both sides gives
1=\widehat{\nu}^{\scriptstyle{t\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}
. Consequently
\widehat{\delta}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}=\mathds{1}
and thus
t\in\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.

Inclusion (
2.2d
) follows from the fact that
P^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}=\delta^{\mathrm{i}{t}}(J_{\varphi}\delta^{\mathrm{i}{t}}J_{\varphi})\widehat{\delta}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\widehat{\delta}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}J_{\widehat{\varphi}})
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
(
[
43
, Theorem 5.20]
).
∎

We obtain more information about the invariants if von Neumann algebra
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
has particularly simple form.

Proposition 2.4
.

If
\Linf(\mathbb{G})=\prod\limits_{i\in{I}}\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
for a family of Hilbert spaces
\{\mathscr{H}_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}
, then
T^{\tau}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{G})=T^{\sigma}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
for
\bullet\in\bigl\{\operatorname{Inn},\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}\bigr\}
.

The above result applies in particular to discrete quantum groups.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Let
\tilde{\tau}_{t}
be the scaling group of
\mathbb{G}
restricted to the center of
\prod\limits_{i\in{I}}\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
which is isomorphic to
\ell^{\infty}(I)
. Each
\tilde{\tau}_{t}
is an automorphism, hence it corresponds to a bijection
I\to{I}
. Since
\{\tilde{\tau}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is point ultraweakly continuous, we conclude that
\tilde{\tau}_{t}=\mathrm{id}
. Let
p_{i}
be the central projection corresponding to
\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})\subset\prod\limits_{j\in{I}}\B(\mathscr{H}_{j})
. For any
x\in\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
we have

\tau{G}_{t}(x)=\tau{G}_{t}(p_{i}x)=\tilde{\tau}_{t}(p_{i})\tau{G}_{t}(x)=p_{i}\tau{G}_{t}(x),

and hence
\tau{G}_{t}
restricts to an automorphism of
\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
. As all automorphisms of
\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
are inner (
[
32
, Corollary 2.9.32]
) we can choose unitary
u_{i,t}\in\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
such that
\bigl.\tau{G}_{t}\bigr|_{\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})}=\Ad(u_{i,t})
. Since the series
\sum\limits_{i\in{I}}u_{i,t}
convergences in the ultraweak topology, we conclude that
\tau{G}_{t}=\Ad\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i\in{I}}u_{i,t}\Bigr)
is inner and
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
. The argument for
\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})
is analogous (alternatively, observe that
\prod\limits_{i\in{I}}\B(\mathscr{H}_{i})
is semifinite).
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Given a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
we have fourteen associated invariants, namely
T^{\circ}_{\bullet\!}(\mathbb{G})
with
\circ\in\{\tau,\sigma\}
and
\bullet\in\bigl\{\,\,,\operatorname{Inn},\operatorname{\overline{Inn}}\bigr\}
,
\Mod(\mathbb{G})
and similar invariants for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. Equality (
2.2a
) and
\Ttau(\mathbb{G})=\Ttau(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
reduces this number to eleven. If
\mathbb{G}
is compact, then propositions
2.3
,
2.4
further reduce this list and all the information is contained in six invariants:

\Ttau(\mathbb{G}),\quad\TtauInn(\mathbb{G}),\quad\TtauAInn(\mathbb{G}),\quad\TsigInn(\mathbb{G}),\quad\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G}),\quad\Mod(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}).

If
\mathbb{G}
is compact and
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
is semifinite (e.g.
\mathbb{G}=G_{q}
or more generally
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is type
\mathrm{I}
, see Sections
3.2
,
4
) then
\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})=\TsigAInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
and the list reduces to four invariants.

3.
Does
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
characterize Kac type?

At the end of
[
23
, Section 5]
we posed two questions, one of which concerns the invariant
\TtauInn
. More precisely for a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
we asked whether
\mathbb{G}
is of Kac type whenever
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
. We also noted that this question will certainly have the negative answer without an additional assumption like that
\mathbb{G}
is second countable (see below). One of the aims of this paper is to show that this question has a positive answer for a broad class of compact quantum groups. Let us formulate the following conjecture:

Conjecture 1
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a second countable compact quantum group. If
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
then
\mathbb{G}
is of Kac type.

Conjecture
1
could be compared with the following famous result in theory of von Neumann algebras. Let
\mathrm{M}
be a von Neumann algebra with separable predual. Then Connes’ invariant
T(\mathrm{M})
equals
\mathbb{R}
if and only if
\mathrm{M}
is semifinite, i.e. admits a tracial n.s.f. weight (
[
37
, Theorem VIII.3.14]
). So far we cannot prove Conjecture
1
in full generality, however, as mentioned above, in subsequent sections we will show its validity for a large class of compact quantum groups.

Remark 3.1
.

As noted in
[
23
, Question 5.32(1)]
, one can easily construct a non-Kac type compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
with
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
. For example, fix
0<q<1
and let
\mathbb{R}_{\text{\tiny{disc}}}
be the group of real numbers with discrete topology, acting on
\Linf(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))
via the scaling automorphisms. Then the bicrossed product
\mathbb{R}_{\text{\tiny{disc}}}\bowtie\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)
is a non-Kac type compact quantum group with all scaling automorphisms inner – it is however not second countable.

3.1.
Quantum groups with a representation
U
such that
2=\dim{U}<\qdim{U}

Consider a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
with an irreducible representation
U
such that
\qdim{U}>\dim{U}
. Then clearly
\mathbb{G}
is not of Kac type and hence
\Ttau(\mathbb{G})\neq\mathbb{R}
. For such compact quantum groups the validity of Conjecture
1
boils down to whether
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
or not. In this section we will demonstrate that the conjecture holds in the case of compact quantum groups which posses a sequence of irreducible representations satisfying certain conditions (listed in Theorem
3.3
below). Later we will show that such a sequence of irreducible representations can be constructed for compact quantum groups with a two dimensional representation whose quantum dimension is strictly greater than two (such a representation must be irreducible).

We begin with a simple lemma which we can prove in the general setting of locally compact quantum groups.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a locally compact quantum group and let
v\in\Linf(\mathbb{H})
be a unitary such that
\tau_{t}{H}=\Ad(v)=v\cdot{v^{*}}
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\sigma^{\varphi}_{s}(v)=\sigma^{\psi}_{s}(v)=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{-ist}v
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\nu_{\mathbb{H}}
is the scaling constant of
\mathbb{H}
. In particular, if
\mathbb{H}
is compact or discrete then
v\in\Linf(\mathbb{H})^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Take
y\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
. Using
\varphi\circ\tau_{-t}^{\mathbb{H}}=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{t}\varphi
(see
[
27
]
) we have

\varphi\bigl((yv)^{*}(yv)\bigr)=\varphi(v^{*}y^{*}yv)=\bigl(\varphi\circ\tau_{-t}^{\mathbb{H}}\bigr)(y^{*}y)=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{t}\varphi(y^{*}y)<+\infty,

and hence
yv\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
. Similarly we check
yv^{*}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
. Take
x\in\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi}
. The above properties imply
xv,vx\in\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi}
. Furthermore

\varphi(vx)=\varphi(vxvv^{*})=\bigl(\varphi\circ\tau_{t}^{\mathbb{H}}\bigr)(xv)=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{-t}\varphi(xv),

hence
[
40
, Lemma 5.4]
gives
\sigma^{\varphi}_{s}(v)=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{-its}v
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
. The second claim can be proved in an analogous way using
\psi\circ\tau^{\mathbb{H}}_{-t}=\nu_{\mathbb{H}}^{t}\psi
. The last statement is implied by the fact that compact and discrete quantum groups have trivial scaling constant.
∎

In what follows we will use the following notation: if
U
is a representation of a compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
and
\uprho_{U}
the associated positive operator describing the modular properties of the Haar measure (
[
28
, Section 1.4]
) then we write

•

\Gamma(U)=\max\operatorname{Sp}\uprho_{U}=\|\uprho_{U}\|
,

•

\gamma(U)=\min\operatorname{Sp}\uprho_{U}=\|\uprho_{U}^{-1}\|^{-1}
.

Note that
\Gamma(\overline{U})=\gamma(U)^{-1}
. Furthermore
\Gamma(U)
and
\gamma(U)
only depend on the (unitary) equivalence class of
U
and hence we may use notation like
\Gamma(\alpha)
and
\gamma(\alpha)
for an equivalence class
\alpha
of representations of
\mathbb{G}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a second countable compact quantum group and let
\{U^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of irreducible representations of
\mathbb{G}
such that

(1)

\Gamma(U^{n})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}+\infty
,

(2)

\inf\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\gamma(U^{n})\qdim{U^{n}}}\,\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{n})}{\qdim{U^{n}}}\Bigr)>0
.

Then
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})\neq\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction: assume that
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R}
. Then by
[
16
, Theorem 0.1]
there exists a strongly continuous one-parameter group
\{v_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of unitary operators in
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
such that
\tau_{t}{G}=\Ad(v_{t})
for all
t
. Set
\varepsilon_{t}=\|v_{t}-\mathds{1}\|_{2}
and note that

(3.1)

\|v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}}-\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}\|_{2}=\bigl\|(v_{t}-\mathds{1})\otimes{v_{t}}+\mathds{1}\otimes(v_{t}-\mathds{1})\bigr\|_{2}\leq{2}\|v_{t}-\mathds{1}\|_{2}=2\varepsilon_{t}.

For each
n
let us fix an orthonormal basis of the carrier Hilbert space of
U^{n}
in which
\uprho_{U^{n}}
is diagonal with eigenvalues
\uprho_{n,1}\leq\dotsm\leq\uprho_{n,d_{n}}
, where
d_{n}=\dim{U^{n}}
. Then the action of the modular group of
{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}
and of the scaling group on the matrix elements of
U^{n}
with respect to this basis is

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \sigma_{t}^{{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}}(U^{n}_{k,l})&=\uprho_{n,k}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U^{n}_{k,l}\uprho_{n,l}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\\
\tau_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}(U^{n}_{k,l})&=\uprho_{n,k}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U^{n}_{k,l}\uprho_{n,l}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\end{aligned}\right.,\qquad{t}\in\mathbb{R}.

(cf. (
1.1
)).

Next, consider the quantum group
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G}
and let
X_{n}=U^{n}_{1,d_{n}}\otimes\overline{U^{n}}_{\!\!\!\!\!\!\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}d_{n},1}\in\Pol(\mathbb{H})
, where the matrix elements of
\overline{U^{n}}
are with respect to the basis dual to the one for the carrier Hilbert space of
U^{n}
. Using the fact that the lowest eigenvalue of
\uprho_{U^{n}}
is
\uprho_{n,1}=\gamma(U^{n})
and the highest one is
\uprho_{n,d_{n}}=\Gamma(U^{n})
and that
\uprho_{\overline{U^{n}}}=(\uprho_{U^{n}}^{-1})^{\top}=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\uprho_{n,1}^{-1},\dotsc,\uprho_{n,d_{n}}^{-1}\bigr)
we find that

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}_{t}(X_{n})&=X_{n}\\
\tau^{\mathbb{H}}_{t}(X_{n})&=\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}X_{n}\end{aligned}\right.,\qquad{t}\in\mathbb{R}

and using orthogonality relations we obtain

\displaystyle\|X_{n}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|X_{n}\Omega{H}\|^{2}={\boldsymbol{h}}{H}(X_{n}^{*}X_{n})

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{h}}{G}\bigl({U^{n}_{1,d_{n}}}^{*}U^{n}_{1,d_{n}}\bigr){\boldsymbol{h}}{G}\bigl({\overline{U^{n}}_{\!\!\!\!\!\!\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}d_{n},1}}^{*}\overline{U^{n}}_{\!\!\!\!\!\!\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}d_{n},1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\left(\uprho_{U^{n}}^{-1}\right)_{1,1}}{\qdim{U^{n}}}\tfrac{\left(\uprho_{\overline{U^{n}}}^{-1}\right)_{d_{n},d_{n}}}{\qdim{\overline{U^{n}}}}=\tfrac{\uprho_{n,1}^{-1}}{\qdim{U^{n}}}\tfrac{\uprho_{n,d_{n}}}{\qdim{U^{n}}}=\tfrac{1}{\gamma(U^{n})\qdim{U^{n}}}\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{n})}{\qdim{U^{n}}}.

Consequently putting
c^{2}=\inf\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\gamma(U^{n})\qdim{U^{n}}}\,\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{n})}{\qdim{U^{n}}}\Bigr)
(recall that by assumption
c>0
) we obtain
\|X_{n}\|_{2}\geq{c}
.

On the other hand

(3.3)

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}-1\bigr|c

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}-1\bigr|\|X_{n}\|_{2}=\bigl\|\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}X_{n}\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\tau_{t}{H}(X_{n})\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|=\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}(v_{t}^{*}\otimes{v_{t}^{*}})\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}(v_{t}^{*}\otimes{v_{t}^{*}})\Omega{H}-(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|+\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\bigr\|\bigl\|(v_{t}^{*}\otimes{v_{t}^{*}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|+\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|.

Now, since
\sigma_{t}^{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})=\sigma_{t}^{{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}}(v_{t})\otimes\sigma_{t}^{{\boldsymbol{h}}{G}}(v_{t})=v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}}
, we have
\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}=(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}
, so

\displaystyle\bigl\|(v_{t}^{*}\otimes{v_{t}^{*}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|=\bigl\|J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\Omega{H}\bigr\|=\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|.

In view of this, the fact that
\|X_{n}\|\leq{1}
, and (
3.1
) the estimate (
3.3
) can be continued as

(3.4)

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}-1\bigr|c

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\bigr\|\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|+\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon_{t}+\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}-X_{n}\Omega{H}\bigr\|.

Now by (
3.2
) we have
X_{n}\in\Linf(\mathbb{H})^{\sigma}
, so

\displaystyle(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})X_{n}\Omega{H}

\displaystyle=J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}

\displaystyle=J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}=J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H},

similarly
X_{n}\Omega_{\mathbb{H}}=J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\Omega_{\mathbb{H}}
and we can continue (
3.4
) as

(3.5)

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\gamma(U^{n})}{\Gamma(U^{n})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}-1\bigr|c

\displaystyle\leq{2}\varepsilon_{t}+\bigl\|J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\Omega{H}\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon_{t}+\|J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}X_{n}^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}{H}}\|\bigl\|(v_{t}\otimes{v_{t}})\Omega{H}-\Omega{H}\bigr\|\leq 4\varepsilon_{t}.

Since
t\mapsto{v_{t}}
is strongly continuous, there exits
t_{1}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{t}<\tfrac{c}{4}
for
t\in\left]0,t_{1}\right]
. Furthermore, as
\Gamma(U^{n})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}+\infty
and
\gamma(U^{n})\leq{1}
, there exists
m
such that
\log\bigl(\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{m})}{\gamma(U^{m})}\bigr)\geq\tfrac{\pi}{2t_{1}}
and thus

t_{2}=\tfrac{\pi}{2}\log\bigl(\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{m})}{\gamma(U^{m})}\bigr)^{-1}

satisfies
t_{2}\in\left]0,t_{1}\right]
and we have
\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{m})}{\gamma(U^{m})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t_{2}}}-1\bigr|=2
. Combining this with (
3.5
) yields the contradiction

2c=\bigl|\bigl(\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{m})}{\gamma(U^{m})}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t_{2}}}-1\bigr|c\leq 4\varepsilon_{t_{2}}<c.\qed

As already mentioned at the beginning of this section, a sequence of irreducible representations satisfying the conditions of Theorem
3.3
can be constructed from a two dimensional representation
U
such that
\qdim{U}>2
(which is automatically irreducible).

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a compact quantum group and let
U
be a representation of
\mathbb{G}
such that
\dim{U}=2<\qdim{U}
. Then there exists a sequence
(U^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of irreducible representations such that

(1)

U^{1}=U
and
U^{n}
is a subrepresentation of a tensor product of copies of
U
and
\overline{U}
,

(2)

\gamma(U^{n})=\gamma(U)^{2n}
,
\Gamma(U^{n})=\Gamma(U)^{2n}
and
\gamma(U^{n})=\Gamma(U^{n})^{-1}
for all
n\geq{2}
,
1
1

1

Let us also mention that the condition
\gamma(V)=\Gamma(V)^{-1}
for any representation
V
is automatic for many compact quantum groups (
[
19
]
).

(3)

\inf\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{n})}{\qdim{U^{n}}}>0
.

We immediately obtain

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a second countable compact quantum group which admits a unitary representation
U
with
\dim{U}=2<\qdim{U}
. Then
\TtauInn(\mathbb{G})\neq\mathbb{R}
.

We start with an auxiliary result of independent interest. The proof of Proposition
3.4
will only use statement (
2
).

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
be compact quantum groups and let
\pi\colon\C^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\mathbb{G})\to\C^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\mathbb{H})
be a unital
*
-homomorphism respecting coproducts. Furthermore let
U\in\C^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\B(\mathscr{H}_{U})
be a finite dimensional unitary representation of
\mathbb{G}
. Then
V=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})U
is a unitary representation of
\mathbb{H}
. Furthermore

(1)

if
V=\bigoplus\limits_{a=1}^{A}V^{a}
is the decomposition of
V
into irreducible representations, then

\bigcup\limits_{a=1}^{A}\operatorname{Sp}\bigl(\uprho_{V^{a}}\otimes\uprho_{V^{a}}^{-1}\bigr)\subset\operatorname{Sp}\bigl(\uprho_{U}\otimes\uprho_{U}^{-1}\bigr),

(2)

if
V
is irreducible, then
\uprho_{V}=\uprho_{U}
.

Remark 3.7
.

The example of
\mathbb{G}=\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
with fundamental representation
U
satisfying
1\not\in\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{U})
,
\mathbb{H}=\{e\}
, and
\pi
equal to the counit shows that
\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{V})\subset\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{U})
can fail.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

It is clear that
V
is a unitary representation of
\mathbb{H}
. Let us decompose
V
into irreducible representations
V=\bigoplus\limits_{a=1}^{A}V^{a}
, and let
\mathscr{H}_{U}=\mathscr{H}_{V}=\bigoplus\limits_{a=1}^{A}\mathscr{H}_{V^{a}}
be the corresponding decomposition of Hilbert spaces. Fix orthonormal bases in the carrier Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}_{U}
and
\mathscr{H}_{V^{1}},\dotsc\mathscr{H}_{V^{A}}
in which the corresponding operators
\uprho_{U},\uprho_{V^{1}},\dotsc,\uprho_{V^{A}}
are diagonal with eigenvalues
\uprho_{U,i}
and
\uprho_{V^{a},j}
(
1\leq{i}\leq\dim{U}
,
1\leq{a}\leq{A}
,
1\leq{j}\leq\dim{V^{a}}
) and let
\bigl\{e^{a}_{k,l}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,1\leq{k,l}\leq\dim{V^{a}}\bigr\}
and
\bigl\{e^{U}_{i,j}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,1\leq{i,j}\leq\dim{U}\bigr\}
be the corresponding matrix units. We have

\sum_{a=1}^{A}\sum_{k,l=1}^{\dim{V^{a}}}V^{a}_{k,l}\otimes{e^{a}_{k,l}}=\bigoplus_{a=1}^{A}V^{a}=V=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})U=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{U}}\pi(U_{i,j})\otimes{e^{U}_{i,j}}.

Applying
\tau_{t}^{\mathbb{H}}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{A}\sum_{k,l=1}^{\dim{V^{a}}}\bigl(\tfrac{\uprho_{V^{a},k}}{\uprho_{V^{a},l}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}V^{a}_{k,l}\otimes{e^{a}_{k,l}}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{\mathbb{H}}_{t}\otimes\mathrm{id})V=\bigl((\tau{H}_{t}\circ\pi)\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)U

\displaystyle=\bigl((\pi\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)U=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{U}}\bigl(\tfrac{\uprho_{U,i}}{\uprho_{U,j}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi(U_{i,j})\otimes{e^{U}_{i,j}}.

Take
\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}
for some
1\leq{a^{\prime}}\leq{A}
,
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{V^{a^{\prime}}})
and let
m
be an invariant mean on
\mathbb{R}
. The above equality implies that for arbitrary
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\Ltwo(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathscr{H}_{U}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a,k,l\text{ such that}}\\
{\uprho_{V^{a},k}/\uprho_{V^{a},l}=\lambda/\lambda^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,(V^{a}_{k,l}\otimes{e^{a}_{k,l}})\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=m\biggl(t\mapsto\sum_{a=1}^{A}\sum_{k,l=1}^{\dim{V^{a}}}\bigl(\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigl(\tfrac{\uprho_{V^{a},k}}{\uprho_{V^{a},l}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,(V^{a}_{k,l}\otimes{e^{a}_{k,l}})\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle}\biggr)

\displaystyle=m\biggl(t\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{U}}\bigl(\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigl(\tfrac{\uprho_{U,i}}{\uprho_{U,j}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\bigl(\pi(U_{i,j})\otimes{e^{U}_{i,j}}\bigr)\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i,j\text{ such that}}\\
{\uprho_{U,i}/\uprho_{U,j}=\lambda/\lambda^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\bigl(\pi(U_{i,j})\otimes{e^{U}_{i,j}}\bigr)\xi^{\prime}\right\rangle}

and hence we can conclude that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a,k,l\text{ such that}}\\
{\uprho_{V^{a},k}/\uprho_{V^{a},l}=\lambda/\lambda^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}V^{a}_{k,l}\otimes{e^{a}_{k,l}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i,j\text{ such that}}\\
{\uprho_{U,i}/\uprho_{U,j}=\lambda/\lambda^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\pi(U_{i,j})\otimes{e^{U}_{i,j}}.

The left hand side is by assumption non-zero, hence so is the right hand side and
\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{U})
.

Ad (
2
). Assume now that
V
is irreducible. To conclude that
\uprho_{V}=\uprho_{U}
, it is enough to show that
\uprho_{U}\in\operatorname{Mor}(V,V^{\text{\rm{\tiny{cc}}}})
and
\Tr(\uprho_{U})=\Tr(\uprho_{U}^{-1})
(
[
28
, Proposition 1.4.4]
). Clearly the second property holds, for the first one recall that
\pi
preserves the antipode (
[
38
, Proposition 1.3.17]
) which allows us to calculate as follows

\displaystyle(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})V

\displaystyle=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl((\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})U\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(U^{\text{\rm{\tiny{cc}}}}(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl((\pi\circ{S^{2}_{\mathbb{G}}})\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(U(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl((S^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\pi)\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)\bigl(U(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})\bigr)=(S^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(V(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U})\bigr)=V^{\text{\rm{\tiny{cc}}}}(\mathds{1}\otimes\uprho_{U}).

This shows that
\uprho_{V}=\uprho_{U}
.
∎

In the proof of Proposition
3.4
,
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
will denote the universal unitary quantum group corresponding to an invertible matrix
F
constructed in
[
44
]
(denoted by
A_{u}(Q)
in that paper). Let us recall that irreducible representations of
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
can be labeled by free product of monoids
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
in such a way that
\alpha
corresponds to the canonical fundamental representation and passing to the conjugate representation corresponds to the unique anti-multiplicative map
x\mapsto\overline{x}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}
satisfying
\overline{\alpha}=\beta
and
\overline{\beta}=\alpha
. Moreover, denoting by
u^{x}
a unitary representation of
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
in the class
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}
the tensor product of representations from the classes
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}
decomposes into irreducible as

(3.6)

u^{x}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{u^{y}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a,b,c\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\\
{x=ac,\>y=\overline{c}b}\end{subarray}}u^{ab}

up to equivalence (cf.
[
3
, Théorème 1]
).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
let
w^{n}_{\delta}
be the word
\delta\overline{\delta}\delta\dotsm\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\star\mathbb{Z}_{+}
(
n
letters). It will be convenient to denote the (class of) the trivial representation by
w^{0}_{\delta}
. The dimension and quantum dimension of
w^{n}_{\delta}
are known
[
25
, Lemma 4.13]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
\delta\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\gamma(w^{2n}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\gamma(\delta)^{n}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n},

\displaystyle\Gamma(w^{2n}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\delta)^{n}\Gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n},

\displaystyle\gamma(w^{2n+1}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\gamma(\delta)^{n+1}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n},

\displaystyle\Gamma(w^{2n+1}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\delta)^{n+1}\Gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n}.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case
\gamma(\alpha)<1
, otherwise
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
is of Kac type and the result holds trivially.

We prove the claim by induction. The equalities (
3.7
) hold for
n=0
, so let us assume their validity for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. We have
w_{\delta}^{2n+1}=w_{\delta}^{2n}\delta
, hence
w_{\delta}^{2n+1}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\overline{\delta}=w_{\delta}^{2n+2}\oplus{w_{\delta}^{2n}}
and

\displaystyle\gamma(\delta)^{n+1}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n+1}

\displaystyle=\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+1})\gamma(\overline{\delta})=\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+1}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\overline{\delta})=\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+2}\oplus{w_{\delta}^{2n}})

\displaystyle=\min\bigl\{\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+2}),\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n})\bigr\}=\min\bigl\{\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+2}),\gamma(\delta)^{n}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n}\bigr\}=\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+2}).

This shows
\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+2})=\gamma(\delta)^{n+1}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n+1}
. Similarly
w_{\delta}^{2n+2}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\delta=w_{\delta}^{2n+3}\oplus{w_{\delta}^{2n+1}}
and, as above, we deduce that
\gamma(w_{\delta}^{2n+3})=\gamma(\delta)^{n+2}\gamma(\overline{\delta})^{n+1}
. The argument for
\Gamma
is analogous.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

By
[
38
, Proposition 6.4.5]
(cf.
[
44
, Theorem 1.3(2)]
) there exists
F\in\operatorname{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
and a unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\colon\C^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+})\to\C^{\text{\tiny{\rm{u}}}}(\mathbb{G})
respecting coproducts and mapping the matrix elements of the fundamental representation
u^{\alpha}
of
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
to the corresponding matrix elements of
U
. Since
\dim{u^{\alpha}}=2
, we have
\gamma(u^{\alpha})=\Gamma(u^{\alpha})^{-1}=\gamma(\overline{u^{\alpha}})
.

Let us introduce a family
\{W^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
of (not necessarily irreducible) representations of
\mathbb{G}
via
W^{n}=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. In particular
W^{0}
is the trivial representation and
W^{1}=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{\alpha}=U
. It is irreducible, hence by Proposition
3.6
(
2
)
\uprho_{W^{1}}=\uprho_{U}=\uprho_{u^{\alpha}}
and consequently

\gamma(U)=\gamma(u^{\alpha}),\quad\Gamma(U)=\Gamma(u^{\alpha}).

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, choose an irreducible subrepresentation
\mathcal{U}^{n}
of
W^{n}
such that
\gamma(\mathcal{U}^{n})=\gamma(W^{n})
. Next, set
U^{1}=\mathcal{U}^{1}=U
and
U^{n}=\mathcal{U}^{2n}
for
n\geq{2}
. We claim that the family
\{U^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
meets the criteria of Proposition
3.4
.

First, as
u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}\subset{u^{\alpha}}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\overline{u^{\alpha}}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\dotsm
(
n
times), we have
\mathcal{U}^{n}\subset{W^{n}}=(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}\subset{U}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\overline{U}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\dotsm
, which shows (
1
). Next, for
n\geq{1}
and
\delta=\alpha
if
n
is even,
\delta=\beta
if
n
is odd, we calculate:

u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{u^{\delta}}=u^{w^{n+1}_{\alpha}}\oplus{u^{w^{n-1}_{\alpha}}}

hence

(3.8)

\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}\bigr)\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{\delta}\bigr)=\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n+1}_{\alpha}}\bigr)\oplus\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n-1}_{\alpha}}\bigr)

and

\displaystyle\gamma(W^{n})\gamma(U)

\displaystyle=\gamma\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}\bigr)\gamma(u^{\delta})=\gamma\Bigl(\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})(u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}})\bigr)\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{\delta}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\gamma\Bigl(\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n+1}_{\alpha}}\bigr)\oplus\bigl((\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})u^{w^{n-1}_{\alpha}}\bigr)\Bigr)=\min\bigl\{\gamma(W^{n+1}),\gamma(W^{n-1})\bigr\}.

As
\gamma(W^{0})=1
and
\gamma(U)<1
, we have by induction
\gamma(W^{n+1})<\gamma(W^{n})
and hence

\gamma(W^{n})\gamma(U)=\gamma(W^{n+1}).

From this we can conclude that

(3.9a)

\gamma(\mathcal{U}^{n})=\gamma(W^{n})=\gamma(U)^{n}=\gamma(u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}),\qquad{n}\geq{1},

in particular

(3.9b)

\gamma(U^{n})=\gamma(\mathcal{U}^{2n})=\gamma(U)^{2n},\qquad{n}\geq{2}

and similarly

(3.9c)

\Gamma(W^{n})=\Gamma(U)^{n}=\Gamma(u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}),\qquad{n}\geq{1}.

A similar inductive reasoning in conjunction with
\qdim{W^{1}}=\qdim{u^{\alpha}}
shows that (
3.8
) implies also

(3.10)

\qdim{W^{n}}=\qdim{u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}}\qquad{n}\in\mathbb{N}.

The next step is to show that
\Gamma(U^{n})=\Gamma(\mathcal{U}^{2n})=\Gamma(W^{2n})
for
n\geq{2}
. For this, we will use
[
19
, Theorem 3.4]
. To that end, for any finite dimensional unitary representation
Z_{0}
of a compact quantum group let us introduce function

P_{Z_{0}}\colon\mathbb{N}\ni{m}\longmapsto\max_{Z\subset{Z_{0}^{\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{m}}}}\dim{Z}\in\mathbb{N},

where the maximum runs over all representations
Z
appearing in the decomposition of
Z_{0}^{\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{m}}
into irreducible representations. Since
\mathcal{U}^{2n}\subset{W^{2n}}
, we have
P_{\mathcal{U}^{2n}}\leq{P_{W^{2n}}}
. Next, because
W^{2n}
is the image of
u^{w^{2n}_{\alpha}}
under
\pi\otimes\mathrm{id}
, we also have
P_{W^{2n}}\leq{P_{u^{w^{2n}_{\alpha}}}}
. The fusion rules in
\operatorname{U}_{F}^{+}
show that any representation
Z
which appears in the decomposition of
(u^{w^{2n}_{\alpha}})^{\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{m}}
is of the form
u^{w^{k}_{\alpha}}
for
1\leq{k}\leq{2nm}
. Thus, using
[
25
, Lemma 4.13]
we obtain

P_{\mathcal{U}^{2n}}(m)\leq{P_{u^{w^{2n}_{\alpha}}}(m)}\leq\max_{1\leq{k}\leq{2nm}}\dim{u^{w^{k}_{\alpha}}}=2nm+1

(remember that we assume
\dim{U}=\dim{u^{\alpha}}=2
). Since the function
P_{\mathcal{U}^{2n}}
is of subexponential growth and by definition
U^{n}=\mathcal{U}^{2n}
,
[
19
, Theorem 3.4]
and equalities (
3.9
) imply

\Gamma(U^{n})=\Gamma(\mathcal{U}^{2n})=\gamma(\mathcal{U}^{2n})^{-1}=\gamma(U)^{-2n}=\Gamma(U)^{2n}=\gamma(U^{n})^{-1},\qquad{n}\in\mathbb{N}.

It is left to check condition (
3
). Set
q=\gamma(U)
and note immediately that
0<q<1
. Since
\dim{U}=2
we have
\qdim{U}=\qdim{u^{\alpha}}=q+q^{-1}
. Equation (
3.10
) and
[
25
, Lemma 4.13]
give

\qdim{\mathcal{U}^{n}}\leq\qdim{W^{n}}=\qdim{u^{w^{n}_{\alpha}}}=[n+1]_{q},

where
[n+1]_{q}=\tfrac{q^{-n-1}-q^{n+1}}{q^{-1}-q}
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
) are the
q
-numbers (see e.g.
[
17
, Section 2.1.1]
or
[
28
, Section 2.4]
). To conclude the proof we calculate for
n\geq{2}

\displaystyle\tfrac{\Gamma(U^{n})}{\qdim{U^{n}}}=\tfrac{\Gamma(\mathcal{U}^{2n})}{\qdim{\mathcal{U}^{2n}}}=\tfrac{1}{\gamma(U)^{2n}\qdim{\mathcal{U}^{2n}}}\geq\tfrac{q^{-1}-q}{q^{2n}(q^{-2n-1}-q^{2n+1})}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\tfrac{q^{-1}-q}{q^{-1}}=1-q^{2}>0.

∎

3.2.
Duals of type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum groups

In this section we prove validity of Conjecture
1
for duals of second countable discrete quantum groups of type
\mathrm{I}
. This class includes all
q
-deformations of compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie groups such as
\operatorname{SU}_{q}(n)
(see Section
4
). Additionally the techniques used in the proof of the main technical result (Theorem
3.9
) owe much to
[
24
,
6
]
and
[
23
, Section 6]
.

For a thorough account of the theory of locally compact quantum groups of type
\mathrm{I}
we refer the reader to
[
21
,
20
,
5
]
, while here we will only list the important objects associated with a second countable discrete quantum group
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
of type
\mathrm{I}
, which we will need in what follows.

Let
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
be a second countable discrete quantum group of type
\mathrm{I}
. Then
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is coamenable (see e.g.
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
) and
\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
, the spectrum of
\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
, is a standard Borel space. There exists a unitary operator

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\colon\ell^{2}(\mathbbl{\Gamma})\longrightarrow{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\operatorname{HS}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

(with
\operatorname{HS}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})
denoting the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators on the carrier Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}
of the representation
\pi
). The unitary
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
implements the isomorphism

\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\ni{x}\longmapsto\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\in{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\bigl(\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

(here we use the isomorphism
\operatorname{HS}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})=\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}
which yields
\B(\operatorname{HS}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi}))=\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\overset{\rule[-1.0pt]{5.0pt}{0.5pt}}{\otimes}\B(\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}})
) and writing
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}
as
{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}x_{\pi}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
the value of the Haar measure
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
on
x
is expressed as follows

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(x)=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr(D_{\pi}^{-2}x_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

for a certain measurable field
\bigl(D_{\pi}\bigr)_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}

of non-singular positive self-adjoint operators.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
be a second countable type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum group. Then

(1)

for any
\alpha\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
we have
\gamma(\alpha)=\Gamma(\alpha)^{-1}
,

(2)

for any
t\in\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
the set
\Lambda_{t}=\bigl\{\Gamma(\beta)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\beta\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\bigr\}\subset\mathbb{T}
is finite.

Proof.

Since
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is compact, we have

+\infty>{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(\mathds{1})=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr(D_{\pi}^{-2})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),

so
D_{\pi}^{-1}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator for
\pi
in some full-measure subset
O_{1}\subset\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
. By rescaling
\mu
we can assume that
\|D_{\pi}^{-1}\|=1
for almost all
\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
. Next we recall from
[
21
, Theorem 5.4]
that

(3.11)

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\nabla_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\mathrm{i}{s}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}\otimes(D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}})^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{s}\in\mathbb{R}.

Returning to the compact quantum group
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
we use standard conventions to fix a unitary representative
U^{\alpha}
of each class
\alpha\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
and we fix an orthonormal basis of the carrier Hilbert space in which
\uprho_{\alpha}
(short-hand for
\uprho_{U^{\alpha}}
) is diagonal with diagonal elements
\uprho_{\alpha,1}\leq\dotsm\leq\uprho_{\alpha,d_{\alpha}}
(similarly to what we did in Section
3.1
, we denote
\dim{U^{\alpha}}
by
d_{\alpha}
). If
\uprho_{\alpha}=\mathds{1}
, there is nothing to prove, so from now on we will only deal with
\alpha
such that
\gamma(\alpha)<1<\Gamma(\alpha)
. For
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{\alpha}\}
write

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{U_{i,j}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

for some measurable field of operators
\bigl(U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\bigr)_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
. Similarly, for
t\in\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
we choose a unitary
v_{t}\in\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
such that
\tau_{t}^{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}=\Ad(v_{t})
and write

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{v_{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}v_{t;\pi}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

for some measurable field
\bigl(v_{t;\pi}\bigr)_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
of unitary operators. Consequently

(3.12)

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\tau_{t}^{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(U_{i,j}^{\alpha})\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}v_{t;\pi}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{v_{t;\pi}^{*}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

and in the same way, from (
3.11
) we get

(3.13)

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\sigma_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(U_{i,j}^{\alpha})\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{s}\in\mathbb{R}.

Now from Lemma
3.2
we know that each
v_{t}
is invariant under the modular group, so in view of (
3.13
) we obtain

{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}v_{t;\pi}D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}v_{t;\pi}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{s}\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence for each
s
there is a full-measure subset of
\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
consisting only of
\pi
such that
D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}v_{t;\pi}D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}=v_{t;\pi}
. Taking the intersection of these subsets over rational
s
and then intersection with
O_{1}
we obtain a subset
O_{2}\subset{O_{1}}
of full measure such that

(3.14)

D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}v_{t;\pi}D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}=v_{t;\pi},\qquad\pi\in{O_{2}},\>s\in\mathbb{R}

(at first this holds only for
s\in\mathbb{Q}
, but then extends to
\mathbb{R}
by continuity). It follows that for
\pi\in{O_{2}}
the operator
v_{t;\pi}
commutes with
f(D_{\pi})
for any bounded measurable function
f
on
\operatorname{Sp}(D_{\pi})
.

Take now
0<a<b
. We will show that for
\pi
in a full-measure subset of
\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
the operator
U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}
shifts the spectral subspace
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(a\leq{D_{\pi}}\leq{b})
as follows:

(3.15)

U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\bigl(\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(a\leq{D_{\pi}}\leq{b})\bigr)\subset\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\bigl((\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}a\leq{D_{\pi}}\leq(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}b\bigr).

Indeed, (
1.1
) and (
3.13
) imply

D_{\pi}^{-2\mathrm{i}{s}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}}=(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\mathrm{i}{s}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha},\qquad{s}\in\mathbb{R}

for
\pi
in a full-measure subset
O_{3,i,j}\subset\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
. Setting
O_{3}=O_{2}\cap\bigcap\limits_{i,j=1}^{d_{\alpha}}O_{3,i,j}
we obtain

U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{D_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}}=\bigl((\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}D_{\pi}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{s}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha},\qquad{s}\in\mathbb{R},\>\pi\in{O_{3}}

with
O_{3}
of full measure. Thus for any Schwartz class function
g

\displaystyle U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{g(\log{D_{\pi}})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{D_{\pi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}}\widehat{g}(t)\,\mathrm{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\bigl((\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}D_{\pi}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\widehat{g}(t)\,\mathrm{d}{t}=g\bigl(\log((\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}D_{\pi})\bigr)U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}.

Approximating the characteristic function of
[\log{a},\log{b}]
pointwise with
g
as above yields

\displaystyle U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\chi(a\leq{D_{\pi}}\leq{b})

\displaystyle=\chi\bigl(a\leq(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}{D_{\pi}}\leq{b}\bigr)U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\chi\bigl((\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}a\leq{D_{\pi}}\leq(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}b\bigr)U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}

and (
3.15
) follows.

Similarly the operators
U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}
also shift eigenspaces of
v_{t;\pi}
.
More precisely if
V
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{T}
then then for almost all
\pi

(3.16)

U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\bigl(\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(v_{t;\pi}\in{V})\bigr)\subset\mathscr{H}(v_{t;\pi}\in\uprho_{\alpha,i}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\uprho_{\alpha,j}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}V)

because (
1.1
) and (
3.12
) imply

v_{t;\pi}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}{v_{t;\pi}^{*}}=\uprho_{\alpha,i}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U_{i,j;\pi}^{\alpha}\uprho_{\alpha,j}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}},\qquad{i,j}\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{\alpha}\},\>\pi\in{O_{4}}

for a full-measure subset
O_{4}\subset{O_{3}}
.

Recall that for
\pi\in{O_{4}}
the operator
D_{\pi}^{-1}
is Hilbert-Schmidt and we have
\|D_{\pi}^{-1}\|=1
. Consequently there exists (finite or infinite, depending on the dimension of
\pi
) sequence
1=q_{1;\pi}>{q_{2;\pi}}>\dotsm
of non-zero positive numbers such that each eigenspace
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}^{-1}=q_{n;\pi})
is finite dimensional and

\mathscr{H}_{\pi}=\bigoplus_{n}\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}^{-1}=q_{n;\pi}).

Equation (
3.14
) implies that
v_{t;\pi}
preserves the decomposition of
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}
into eigenspaces of
D_{\pi}^{-1}
. In particular
v_{t;\pi}
preserves the finite dimensional eigenspace
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}^{-1}=1)=\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)
which consequently decomposes as

\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{K}\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(v_{t;\pi}=\mu_{k})\cap\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)

for some
\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{T}
. By (
3.15
) and (
3.16
)

(3.17)

\displaystyle U_{i,d_{\alpha};\pi}^{\alpha}\bigl(\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(v_{t;\pi}=\mu_{k})

\displaystyle\cap\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\subset\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\bigl(v_{t;\pi}=\uprho_{\alpha,i}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\Gamma(\alpha)^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\mu_{k}\bigr)\cap\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\Bigl(D_{\pi}=\bigl(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\Gamma(\alpha)\bigr)^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}\Bigr).

Finally let us note that for each
\beta\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
we have
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d_{\beta}}{U_{i,d_{\beta}}^{\beta}}^{*}U_{i,d_{\beta}}^{\beta}=\mathds{1}
which implies that

(3.18)

\sum_{i=1}^{\dim{\beta}}{U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}}^{*}U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}=\mathds{1}

for all
\pi
in some full-measure subset
O_{5}\subset{O_{4}}
and all
\beta\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
(recall that
\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is countable).

We are now ready to prove the two claims of our theorem. If
\gamma(\alpha)>\Gamma(\alpha)^{-1}
then for any
i\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{\alpha}\}

\uprho_{\alpha,i}\Gamma(\alpha)\geq\gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)>1,

so that
\bigl(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\Gamma(\alpha)\bigr)^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}<1
, and consequently

\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\Bigl(D_{\pi}=\bigl(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\Gamma(\alpha)\bigr)^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}\Bigr)=\{0\}

because all eigenvalues of
D_{\pi}
are greater or equal to
1
(as we have
\|D_{\pi}^{-1}\|=1
). This, however, contradicts the combination of (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) for
\beta=\alpha
. Similarly, if
\gamma(\alpha)<\Gamma(\alpha)^{-1}
then the equality
\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{\overline{\alpha}})=\operatorname{Sp}(\uprho_{\alpha})^{-1}
leads to a contradiction with (
3.18
) for
\beta=\overline{\alpha}
which ends the proof of claim (
1
).

Ad (
2
). Fix
\pi\in{O_{5}}
and take an arbitrary
\beta\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
,
i\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{\beta}\}
, and let
\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{K}
be eigenvalues of
v_{t;\pi}
on
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)
. Furthermore let
\xi_{k}
be a non-zero element of
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(v_{t;\pi}=\mu_{k})\cap\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1)
.

If
\uprho_{\beta,i}>\gamma(\beta)
then
\bigl(\uprho_{\beta,i}\Gamma(\beta)\bigr)^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}<1
and hence, by the same argument as above, we have

U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}\xi_{k}=0.

It follows that

0\neq\xi_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim{\beta}}{U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}}^{*}U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}\xi_{k}=\sum_{\uprho_{\beta,i}=\gamma(\beta)}{U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}}^{*}U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}\xi_{k}

and hence there exists
i
such that
\uprho_{\beta,i}=\gamma(\beta)=\Gamma(\beta)^{-1}
(by (
1
)) and

0\neq{U_{i,d_{\beta};\pi}^{\beta}}\xi_{k}\in\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\bigl(v_{t;\pi}=\Gamma(\beta)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\mu_{k}\bigr)\cap\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(D_{\pi}=1).

Thus
\Gamma(\beta)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\mu_{k}
is an eigenvalue of
v_{t;\pi}
, so
\Gamma(\beta)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}=\mu_{l}\mu_{k}^{-1}
for some
l
. Consequently it belongs to the finite set

\operatorname{Sp}\bigl(\chi(D_{\pi}=1)v_{t;\pi}\bigr)\cdot\operatorname{Sp}\bigl(\chi(D_{\pi}=1)v_{t;\pi}\bigr)^{-1}.\qed

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
be a second countable type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum group,
\alpha\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
and
t\in\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. Then the number
\Gamma(\alpha)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}
is a root of unity.

Proof.

Suppose
\Gamma(\alpha)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}
is not a root of unity for some
\alpha
and some
t\in\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. Then setting
\alpha_{n}
to be the class of the subrepresentation of
(U^{\alpha})^{\text{\tiny$\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.40282pt\hskip-3.40282pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-3.40282pt\hskip-2.70836pt\raisebox{-0.85208pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\top$}$}}\hskip-2.70836pt\hskip 3.40282pt}}$}{n}}
such that
\Gamma(\alpha_{n})=\Gamma(\alpha)^{n}
, we obtain an infinite sequence of different numbers
\bigl(\Gamma(\alpha_{n})^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\bigl(\Gamma(\alpha)^{2\mathrm{i}{nt}}\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
belonging to the finite set
\Lambda_{t}
described in statement (
2
) of Theorem
3.9
.
∎

Corollary
3.10
provides a simple proof that Conjecture
1
holds for duals of second countable discrete quantum groups of type
\mathrm{I}
:

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
be a second countable type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum group. If
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is not of Kac type then
\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
is countable. In particular
\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})=\mathbb{R}
implies that
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is of Kac type.

Proof.

Assume that
\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
is uncountable and
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is not of Kac type. Next take
\alpha\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
such that
\Gamma(\alpha)>1
and consider the set
S_{\alpha}=\bigl\{\Gamma(\alpha)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,t\in\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\bigr\}
. Since
\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
is an uncountable subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the set

\TtauInn(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\cap\left[\tfrac{k\pi}{\log{\Gamma(\alpha)}},\tfrac{(k+1)\pi}{\log{\Gamma(\alpha)}}\right[

is also uncountable. Since the map
t\mapsto\Gamma(\alpha)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}
is injective on this set, we find that
S_{\alpha}
is uncountable as well. This, however, contradicts the fact that, by Corollary
3.10
, all elements of
S_{\alpha}
are roots of unity.
∎

4.
Invariants of
q
-deformations of a compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie groups

In this section we will compute the invariants introduced in Section
2
for
q
-deformations of compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie groups. The main tool will be a precise description of the action of the scaling group of the
q
-deformation
G_{q}
in terms of the direct integral decomposition of
\Linf(G_{q})
over the maximal torus
T
(Theorem
4.7
). The necessary prerequisite for this is the description of Plancherel objects for the dual
\widehat{G_{q}}
of
G_{q}
which is carried out in Section
4.2
based on some general results from Section
4.1
.

4.1.
Preliminaries

We begin by recalling the definition of
G_{q}
and its basic properties. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the complexified Lie algebra of a compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie group
G
,
T
a maximal torus of
G
with complexified Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
,
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
a positive multiple of the form on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
obtained from the Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}
and
W
the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
with longest element
w_{\circ}
. We assume the normalization
\langle\alpha|\alpha\rangle=2
for short roots
\alpha
. By
\Phi\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
we denote the root system,
\Phi^{+}\subset\Phi
the set of positive roots, and by
\{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{r}\}\subset\Phi^{+}
the set of simple roots (so that
r
is the rank of
\mathfrak{g}
). Next,
{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi
is the root lattice,
\widehat{T}\cong{\boldsymbol{P}}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
the weight lattice,
{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}\subset{\boldsymbol{P}}
the positive cone, and
\varpi_{1},\dotsc,\varpi_{r}\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
are the fundamental weights, which by definition satisfy
2\tfrac{\langle\varpi_{i}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
. Finally,

\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varpi_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}

is the Weyl vector.

For details of the construction of
G_{q}
we refer to
[
28
]
– we will however follow the conventions of
[
9
,
31
]
(which differ from
[
28
]
) as we want to use results from these papers. Here we will only outline some of the aspects of the construction. The construction depends on the deformation parameter
q\in\left]0,1\right[
. Having fixed
q
one first constructs the Hopf
*
-algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, which is a deformation of the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. As a unital
*
-algebra,
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is generated by elements
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i},K_{i}^{-1}
(
1\leq{i}\leq{r}
) satisfying the following relations:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}lr@{\;=\;}l}K_{i}K_{i}^{-1}&K_{i}^{-1}K_{i}=\mathds{1},&K_{i}K_{j}&K_{j}K_{i},\\[3.0pt]
K_{i}E_{j}&q^{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}E_{j}K_{i},&K_{i}F_{j}&q^{-\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}F_{j}K_{i},\end{array}\\[12.0pt]
E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\tfrac{K_{i}^{-1}-K_{i}}{q_{i}^{-1}-q_{i}},\\[3.0pt]
\left.\begin{array}[]{l@{\;=\;}r}\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}\begin{bmatrix}1-a_{i,j}\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q_{i}}E_{i}^{k}E_{j}E_{i}^{1-a_{i,j}-k}&0\\
\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}\begin{bmatrix}1-a_{i,j}\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q_{i}}F_{i}^{k}F_{j}F_{i}^{1-a_{i,j}-k}&0\end{array}\right\}\qquad{i}\neq{j}\end{array}

with
q_{i}=q^{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle/2}
,
a_{i,j}=2\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}
(these are the entries of the Cartan matrix), and

\begin{bmatrix}m\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q}
denoting the
q
-binomial coefficients
\tfrac{[m]_{q}!}{[m-k]_{q}![k]_{q}!}
, where
[m]_{q}!=[m]_{q}[m-1]_{q}\dotsm[1]_{q}
,
[m]_{q}=\tfrac{q^{-m}-q^{m}}{q^{-1}-q}
.

Next we define comultiplication and
*
-structure on
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
via

\widehat{\Delta}(K_{i})=K_{i}\otimes{K_{i}},\quad\widehat{\Delta}(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes{K_{i}}+\mathds{1}\otimes{E_{i}},\quad\widehat{\Delta}(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes\mathds{1}+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes{F_{i}}

and

K_{i}^{*}=K_{i},\quad E_{i}^{*}=F_{i}K_{i},\quad F_{i}^{*}=K_{i}^{-1}E_{i}.

It is known that
\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),\widehat{\Delta}\bigr)
is a Hopf
*
-algebra with antipode and counit

\displaystyle\widehat{\varepsilon}(K_{i})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\widehat{\varepsilon}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\widehat{\varepsilon}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\widehat{S}(K_{i})

\displaystyle=K_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle\widehat{S}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle\widehat{S}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=-K_{i}F_{i}

for
1\leq{i}\leq{r}
. For
\alpha=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}\alpha_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
we will use the shorthand notation
K_{\alpha}=K_{1}^{n_{1}}\dotsm{K_{r}^{n_{r}}}
.

One defines
\Pol(G_{q})\subset\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
as the linear span of matrix coefficients of all
admissible
, finite dimensional
*
-representations of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
(roughly speaking, these are the representations in which all
K_{i}
are diagonalizable, see e.g.
[
28
, Definition 2.4.3]
). By construction,
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
embeds into
\Pol(G_{q})^{*}
.

As suggested by the notation,
\Pol(G_{q})
indeed turns out to be a C.Q.G. Hopf algebra, i.e. a Hopf
*
-algebra associated to a compact quantum group. The set
\Irr{G_{q}}
of equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
G_{q}
can be identified with
{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and for any
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}=\Irr{G_{q}}
we will denote by
\pi_{\varpi}
the associated
*
-representation of the
*
-algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
whose dimension will be denoted by
\dim{\varpi}
. These identifications are described by equality
\pi_{\varpi}(\omega)=(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})U^{\varpi}
valid for
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
\omega\in\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. One defines the
weights of
\varpi
as these
\mu\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
for which space

\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu)=\bigl\{\xi\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\pi_{\varpi}(K_{i})\xi=q^{\langle\mu|\alpha_{i}\rangle}\xi,\>1\leq{i}\leq{r}\bigr\}

is non-zero. For example, any
\varpi
is a weight of
\varpi
in this sense. Moreover we have the equality
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}=\bigoplus\limits_{\mu}\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu)
with
\mu
running over the weights of
\varpi
.

Whenever
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
\xi\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu),\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu^{\prime})
are vectors of weights
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
, we define
left
and
right weights
of the matrix element
U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\xi,\eta})U^{\varpi}\in\Pol(G_{q})
by

\lwt\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)=\wt(\xi)=\mu,\quad\rwt\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)=\wt(\eta)=\mu^{\prime}.

In formulas like these, we will always implicitly assume that
\xi,\eta
have well defined weights. Next, we extend definition of
\lwt,\rwt
in the obvious way, so that we obtain
{\boldsymbol{P}}
-gradings of
\Pol(G_{q})
. Let us also fix an orthonormal basis
\{\xi^{\varpi}_{1},\dotsc,\xi^{\varpi}_{\dim{\varpi}}\}
of
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
in which each vector
\xi^{\varpi}_{i}
has a well defined weight.

One can choose unit vectors
\xi_{\varpi}\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\varpi)
and
\eta_{w_{\circ}\varpi}\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(w_{\circ}\varpi)
of highest (resp. lowest) weight so that the corresponding elements

b_{\varpi}=U^{\varpi}(\xi_{\varpi},\eta_{w_{\circ}\varpi})\in\Pol(G_{q}),\qquad\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}

have convenient properties – for details see
[
9
, Section 5]
. These elements (especially
b_{\rho}
) will play an important role in what follows. One can obtain detailed information about these elements – see equation (
4.23
). In particular, one can calculate that in the case of
G_{q}=\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)
we have
b_{N}=(-\gamma)^{N}
for all
N\in\Irr(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))=\mathbb{Z}_{+}
(where
\gamma
is one of the standard generators of
\Pol(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))
, cf.
[
46
, Section 1]
).

Irreducible representations of the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\C(G_{q})
were classified by Korogodski and Soibelman (
[
18
]
) and the Haar measure on
G_{q}
was described by Reshetikhin and Yakimov in
[
31
]
. Let us now briefly describe some of these results in the conventions of
[
9
]
.

Let
\theta_{q}\colon\C(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))\to\B(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+}))
be the representation defined by

\left.\begin{array}[]{c@{\;=\;}l}\theta_{q}(\alpha)e_{n}&\sqrt{1-q^{2n}}e_{n-1}\\
\theta_{q}(\gamma)e_{n}&q^{n}e_{n}\end{array}\right\}\qquad{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

where
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
is the standard orthonormal basis of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})
and
e_{-1}=0
. For
1\leq{i}\leq{r}
let

\rho_{i}\colon\C(\operatorname{SU}_{q_{i}}(2))\longrightarrow\C(G_{q})

be the map corresponding to the embedding
\mathcal{U}_{q_{i}}(\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C}))\to\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. By taking compositions of these maps we obtain representations of
\C(G_{q})
defined as

\pi_{s_{i}}\colon\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.95955pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.95955pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\C(G_{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.08372pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.95955pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.95955pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\C\bigl(\operatorname{SU}_{q_{i}}(2)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 101.75665pt\raise 6.73561pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.1255pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\theta_{q_{i}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 121.3784pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 121.3784pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\B\bigl(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})\bigr),}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

where
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}
denote the generators of the Weyl group
W
. For any
w\in{W}\setminus\{e\}
choose a reduced presentation
w=s_{1}\dotsm{s_{\ell(w)}}
and define

\pi_{w}=\pi_{s_{1}}\star\dotsm\star\pi_{s_{\ell(w)}}=(\pi_{s_{1}}\otimes\dotsm\otimes\pi_{s_{\ell(w)}})\circ\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(\ell(w)-1)}\colon\C(G_{q})\longrightarrow\B\bigl(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})^{\otimes\ell(w)}\bigr).

(where
\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(n)}
is defined inductively as
\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(1)}=\Delta_{G_{q}}
,
\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(k+1)}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\dotsm\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{G_{q}})\circ\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(k)}
).
The representation
\pi_{w}
is irreducible and up to equivalence it does not depend on expression of
w
as a word in the generators.

Characters of
\C(G_{q})
are in bijection with the torus
T
. For
\lambda\in{T}
, the corresponding character is given by

\pi_{\lambda}\colon\C(G_{q})\ni{U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)}\longmapsto{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\eta\right\rangle}\langle\wt(\xi),\lambda\rangle={\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\eta\right\rangle}\langle\wt(\eta),\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{C}.

Korogodski and Soibelman showed that irreducible representations of
\C(G_{q})
are (up to equivalence) precisely
2
2

2

We use representations
\pi_{\lambda}\star\pi_{w}
(rather than
\pi_{w}\star\pi_{\lambda}
) to be in agreement with
[
9
]
. This choice gives an equivalent parametrization of the spectrum, see
[
18
, Proposition 6.2.6]
.

\bigl\{\pi_{\lambda,w}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T},\>w\in{W}\bigr\}

where
\pi_{\lambda,e}=\pi_{\lambda}
(
\lambda\in{T}
) and

\pi_{\lambda,w}=\pi_{\lambda}\star\pi_{w}=(\pi_{\lambda}\otimes\pi_{w})\Delta_{G_{q}},\qquad\lambda\in{T},\>w\in{W}\setminus\{e\}.

Furthermore,
\pi_{\lambda,w}
and
\pi_{\lambda^{\prime},w^{\prime}}
are non-equivalent for
(\lambda,w)\neq(\lambda^{\prime},w^{\prime})
. Consequently the spectrum of
\C(G_{q})
can be identified with
T\times{W}
as a set. For
x=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
where
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
have well defined weights, and
(\lambda,w)\in{T}\times{W}
we have the following useful formula:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{\lambda,w}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}\pi_{\lambda}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\xi^{\varpi}_{i})\bigr)\pi_{w}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi^{\varpi}_{i},\eta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\xi^{\varpi}_{i}\right\rangle}\langle\wt(\xi),\lambda\rangle\,\pi_{w}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi^{\varpi}_{i},\eta)\bigr)=\langle\lwt(x),\lambda\rangle\pi_{w}(x).

It is well-known to the experts that
\C(G_{q})
is a type
\mathrm{I}

\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra with the obvious measurable structure on its spectrum. However we could not precisely locate a complete proof in the literature, so we record it here for the reader’s convenience. We also show that the field of representations
(\pi_{\lambda,w})_{(\lambda,w)\in{T}\times{W}}
is measurable – a result needed for concrete calculations. Note that it follows from the fact that
\C(G_{q})
is type
\mathrm{I}
that
G_{q}
is coamenable (
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
, see also
[
28
, Theorem 2.7.14]
).

Lemma 4.1
.

(1)

The
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\C(G_{q})
is separable, unital and type
\mathrm{I}
.

(2)

The Mackey-Borel structure on its spectrum is equal to the natural Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
T\times{W}
.

(3)

For
w\in{W}\setminus\{e\}
fix a unitary operator
