U_{w}\colon\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})^{\otimes\ell(w)}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
, where
\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
is the standard Hilbert space of dimension
\boldsymbol{n}=\dim{\pi_{w}}=\aleph_{0}
, set also
U_{e}=1
. Then the map
[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\mapsto{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U^{*}_{w}}
between the spectrum of
\C(G_{q})
and the space of irreducible representations, is measurable.

Proof.

In this proof we will write
\operatorname{Sp}{\C(G_{q})}
for the spectrum of
\C(G_{q})
(i.e. the set of equivalence classes of irreducible representations) equipped with its Mackey-Borel structure (
[
11
, Section 3.8]
) and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
for the set of irreducible representations acting on standard Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
of dimension
\boldsymbol{n}\leq\aleph_{0}
, equipped with Borel
\sigma
-algebra (see
[
11
, Section 3.8]
). We will also use measurable subspaces
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})
of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
consisting of representations of dimension
\boldsymbol{n}
. Both
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
are standard (
[
11
, Proposition 3.7.4]
). Throughout the proof, we will distinguish a representation
\pi
from its class
[\pi]
. Recall that the Mackey-Borel structure is the quotient
\sigma
-algebra for the natural surjection
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}{\C(G_{q})}
.

By results of Korogodski and Soibelman, any irreducible representation of
\C(G_{q})
is equivalent to
\pi_{\lambda,w}
for some
(\lambda,w)\in{T}\times{W}
. Furthermore,
[
31
, Proposition 4.3]
shows that the image of each representation
\pi_{\lambda,w}
contains a non-zero compact operator, hence
[
32
, Definition 4.6.3, Theorem 4.6.4]
implies that
\C(G_{q})
is a type
\mathrm{I}

\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra. Consequently,
\operatorname{Sp}{\C(G_{q})}
is also standard (
[
11
, Proposition 4.6.1]
).

Fix
w\in{W}
. We first claim that the corresponding torus
\bigl\{[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}
is measurable in
\operatorname{Sp}{\C(G_{q})}
or, equivalently, that
\bigl\{\rho\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\exists\>\lambda\in{T}\;[\rho]=[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\bigr\}
is Borel in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
. To proceed, we will use the family of ideals
I_{w}\subset\C(G_{q})
(
w\in{W}
) introduced in
[
18
, Equation (5.1.1)]
. By
[
18
, Theorem 5.3.3]
, for any
\lambda\in{T}
and
w^{\prime}\in{W}
we have that
I_{w^{\prime}}\subset\ker\pi_{\lambda,w}
if and only if
w^{\prime}=w
. Let
\boldsymbol{n}=\dim{\pi_{w}}
and choose countable dense subsets
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
and
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
I_{w}
. We have that

\displaystyle\bigl\{\rho\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\exists\>\lambda\in{T}\;[\rho]=[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\rho\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\forall\>{x}\in{I_{w}}\;\rho(x)=0\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i,j,k,m=1}^{\infty}\Bigl\{\rho\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})\,\Bigr|\Bigl.\,\bigl|{\left\langle{\xi_{i}}\,\vline\,\rho(x_{k})\xi_{j}\right\rangle}\bigr|\leq\tfrac{1}{m}\Bigr\}

which by definition is measurable in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
.

Next we show that for each fixed
w\in{W}
, the set
\bigl\{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}
is measurable in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
or, equivalently, in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})
where
\boldsymbol{n}=\dim{\pi_{w}}
. Consider the injective function
T\ni\lambda\mapsto{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}}\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})
. Since both
T
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}_{\boldsymbol{n}\!\!}\C(G_{q})
are standard, it is enough to argue that this map is measurable (
[
11
, Appendix B 21]
). To that end take
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{n}}
and
x\in\C(G_{q})
– we need to show that
f\colon{T}\ni\lambda\mapsto{\left\langle{\zeta}\,\vline\,U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}(x)U_{w}^{*}\zeta^{\prime}\right\rangle}\in\mathbb{C}
is measurable. As any pointwise limit of a sequence of measurable functions is measurable, it is enough to consider the case of
x=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
for
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
with well defined weights. For
\lambda\in{T}
we have
\pi_{\lambda,w}(x)=\pi_{w}(x)\langle\lwt(x),\lambda\rangle
(equation (
4.1
)), which implies that
f
is measurable. This shows that
\bigl\{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}
is measurable in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{Irr}}\C(G_{q})
and consequently is standard (
[
11
, Appendix B 20]
). Then the canonical map

(4.2)

\bigl\{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}\ni{U_{w}}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}\longmapsto[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\in\bigl\{[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}

is a measurable bijection between standard Borel spaces, and hence it is an isomorphism and its inverse
[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\mapsto{U_{w}^{*}}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}
is measurable (
[
11
, Appendix B 22]
). Upon taking the union over all
w
, we obtain (
3
).

Finally let us fix
w\in{W}
. The the composition

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigl\{U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigl\{[\pi_{\lambda,w}]\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}}

of the map taking
\lambda
to
U_{w}\pi_{\lambda,w}U_{w}^{*}
and then (
4.2
)
is a measurable isomorphism which proves (
2
).
∎

Reshetikhin and Yakimov (
[
31
]
, see also
[
9
, Theorem 9.11]
) showed that the Haar measure on
G_{q}
is given by

(4.3)

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}(x)=\bigl(\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\bigl(1-q^{2\langle\rho|\alpha\rangle}\bigr)\bigr)\int\limits_{T}\Tr\bigl(\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(x|b_{\rho}|^{2})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda},\qquad{x}\in\C(G_{q}),

where
\mathrm{d}\lambda
is the normalized Lebesgue measure on
T
. From now on we equip the spectrum of
\C(G_{q})
with the normalized Lebesgue measure on
\bigl\{\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\lambda\in{T}\bigr\}\cong{T}
, so that
\operatorname{Sp}{\C(G_{q})}=T
as measure spaces. Equation (
4.1
) implies

(4.4)

\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)=\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr),\qquad{\lambda}\in{T}.

Consequently, since
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}(\mathds{1})=1
it follows from (
4.3
) that
\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(b_{\rho})\neq{0}
for all
\lambda\in{T}
.

Our next goal is to look at
\widehat{G_{q}}
as a type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum group and describe its Plancherel objects (
[
20
, Section 3]
). The main ingredient is formula (
4.3
), but there is a number of properties to be checked. It will be convenient to consider a more general situation: let
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
be a second countable, type
\mathrm{I}
discrete quantum group. Then
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
is coamenable (
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
) and
\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}=\operatorname{Sp}{\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})}
is a standard Borel space. Equip it with the standard measurable field of Hilbert spaces
(\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}})_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
, i.e.
\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathbb{C}^{\dim{\boldsymbol{x}}}
. Then
\bigl(\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}})\bigr)_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is also a measurable field of Hilbert spaces (
[
12
, Part II, Chapter 1]
). We fix a measurable field of representations
(\pi_{\boldsymbol{x}})_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
such that the class of
\pi_{\boldsymbol{x}}
is equal to
\boldsymbol{x}
(
[
11
, Section 8.6.1]
) – from now on we will abuse notation and identify
\pi_{\boldsymbol{x}}
with
\boldsymbol{x}
, so that e.g. a field of vectors will be denoted by
(\xi_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
. Let us denote the Haar measure on
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
by
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
. Assume that
\mu
is a probability measure on
\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
,
(a_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is a measurable field of positive, bounded operators on
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}
such that
a_{\pi}\neq{0}
for almost all
\pi
, and

(4.5)

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(x)=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\bigl(\pi(x)a_{\pi}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{x}\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}).

In this situation we have the following:

Proposition 4.2
.

(1)

For almost all
\pi
, the operator
a_{\pi}
is Hilbert-Schmidt with trivial kernel,

(2)

equation (
4.5
) holds for all
x\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
,

(3)

there is a unitary operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\colon\Ltwo(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\to{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
satisfying

(4.6)

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(x)={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(x)a_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{x}\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}),

(4)

\bigl(\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},(a_{\pi}^{-1})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}},\mu\bigr)
are the left Plancherel objects for
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
.

Statement (
4
) of Proposition
4.2
means that
\bigl(\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},(a_{\pi}^{-1})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}},\mu\bigr)
satisfy all the properties of
[
20
, Theorem 3.3]
. As an immediate corollary we obtain

Corollary 4.3
.

For all
\lambda\in{T}
, the operator
\pi_{w_{\circ},\lambda}(|b_{\rho}|)
is Hilbert-Schmidt with trivial kernel and

\Biggl(\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},\biggl(\Bigl(\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\bigl(1-q^{2\langle\rho|\alpha\rangle}\bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr)\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-1}\biggr)_{\lambda\in{T}},\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\Biggr)

are the left Plancherel objects for
\widehat{G_{q}}
.

As we already noted in equation (
4.4
), we have
\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(|b_{\rho}|)=\pi_{w_{\circ}}(|b_{\rho}|)
for all
\lambda\in{T}
. Nonetheless, we will keep writing
\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(|b_{\rho}|)
whenever we want to highlight that we see this operator as acting on
\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Setting
x=\mathds{1}
in (
4.5
) gives
1=\int\limits_{\Irr\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\Tr(a_{\pi}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
, hence
a_{\pi}
is Hilbert-Schmidt for almost all
\pi
. Then it follows by an approximation that (
4.5
) holds for all
x\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
.

Now we want to show that
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
can be defined by (
4.6
). Take
x\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
and let us argue that
(\pi(x)a_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is a measurable field of Hilbert-Schmidt operators. Clearly for almost all
\pi
, the operator
\pi(x)a_{\pi}
is Hilbert-Schmidt and by definition, the field of operators
(\pi(x))_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is measurable. Take measurable vector fields
(\xi_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
,
(\eta_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
. The function

\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\ni\pi\longmapsto{\left\langle{\xi_{\pi}\otimes\overline{\eta_{\pi}}}\,\vline\,\pi(x)a_{\pi}\right\rangle}=\Tr\bigl({\left|\eta_{\pi}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\xi_{\pi}\right|}\,\pi(x)a_{\pi}\bigr)={\left\langle{\xi_{\pi}}\,\vline\,\pi(x)a_{\pi}\eta_{\pi}\right\rangle}\in\mathbb{C}

is measurable and hence by
[
13
, Proposition 7.20]
, the field
(\pi(x)a_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
of Hilbert-Schmidt operators is measurable. Consequently, equation (
4.5
) allows us to define the operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
on
\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}))
via (
4.6
). Since it is an isometry, it extends to the whole of
\Ltwo(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
.

In the next step we show that
a_{\pi}
has trivial kernel for almost all
\pi
. Take
x\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
and
y\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. Then

(4.7)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\bigl(\pi(y)a_{\pi}^{2}\pi(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\bigl(\pi(xy)a_{\pi}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)={\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(xy)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\bigl(y\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{-\mathrm{i}}(x)\bigr)=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\Bigl(\pi(y)\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{-\mathrm{i}}(x)\bigr)a_{\pi}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi).

We can interpret equation (
4.7
) as equality of two normal bounded functionals:

(4.8)

{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\Tr\bigl(\,\cdot\;a_{\pi}^{2}\pi(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\quad\text{and}\quad{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\Tr\Bigl(\,\cdot\;\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{-\mathrm{i}}(x)\bigr)a_{\pi}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

on operators of the form
{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}\pi(y)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\in{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
with
y\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. The density result
[
11
, Proposition 8.6.4]

(4.9)

\Biggl\{\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(z)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\,\biggr|\biggl.\,z\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\Biggr\}^{\prime\prime}=\operatorname{Dec}\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathscr{H}_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

implies that functionals (
4.8
) are equal and consequently

a_{\pi}^{2}\pi(x)=\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{-\mathrm{i}}(x)\bigr)a_{\pi}^{2}

holds for almost all
\pi
. Fix such a
\pi
and assume additionally that
\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{t}(x)=\gamma^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}x
, (
t\in\mathbb{R}
) for some
\gamma>0
. Then
a_{\pi}^{2}\pi(x)=\pi(x)(\gamma{a_{\pi}})^{2}
and by induction

(4.10)

a_{\pi}^{2k}\pi(x)=\pi(x)(\gamma{a_{\pi}})^{2k},\qquad{k}\in\mathbb{N}.

and consequently
P(a_{\pi}^{2})\pi(x)=\pi(x)P(\gamma^{2}{a_{\pi}}^{2})
for any polynomial
P
. Now if
\operatorname{Sp}(a_{\pi}^{2})=\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dotsc\}
and
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\dotsm
then taking
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to be a sequence of polynomials such that
P_{n}(0)=1
and
\bigl|P_{n}(\lambda_{k})\bigr|\leq\tfrac{1}{n}
for
k\leq{n}
and
|P_{n}|\leq{M}
(for some constant
M
) on
\bigl[0,\|a_{\pi}^{2}\|\bigr]
yields

P_{n}(a_{\pi}^{2})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\chi(a_{\pi}^{2}=0)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{n}(\gamma^{2}a_{\pi}^{2})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\chi(\gamma^{2}a_{\pi}^{2}=0)

strongly. It follows that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\pi}=0)\pi(x)

\displaystyle=\chi(a_{\pi}^{2}=0)\pi(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}(a_{\pi}^{2})\pi(x)

\displaystyle=\pi(x)\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}(\gamma^{2}a_{\pi}^{2})=\pi(x)\chi(\gamma^{2}a_{\pi}^{2}=0)=\pi(x)\chi(a_{\pi}=0)

(the limits are in the strong topology).

The assumption
\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{t}(x)=\gamma^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}x
for some
\gamma>0
is satisfied by all matrix elements of irreducible representations of
\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
(in appropriate bases). Consequently, by linearity and continuity, equation (
4.11
) holds for all
x\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
and almost all
\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
(recall that
\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
is separable). It follows that
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}(a_{\pi}=0)
is an invariant subspace for
\pi
– since
\pi
is irreducible and
a_{\pi}\neq{0}
, we have
\mathscr{H}(a_{\pi}=0)=\{0\}
. Consequently
a_{\pi}
has trivial kernel for almost all
\pi\in\Irr(\mathbbl{\Gamma})
.

Next we claim that
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
is surjective. If this is not the case, there is a norm
1
vector
T={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}T_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
in
{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
which is orthogonal to the image of
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
. In particular

0=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\bigl(T_{\pi}^{*}\pi(x)a_{\pi}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\qquad{x}\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}).

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\Omega_{N}
be the measurable subset of those
\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
for which
\|T_{\pi}\|_{\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})}^{2}\leq{N}
. By (
4.9
) and the Kaplansky density theorem, there exists a bounded sequence
(x_{n,N})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
such that

{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(x_{n,N})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\chi_{\Omega_{N}}(\pi)T_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

in the strong topology. Indeed, since
(T_{\pi})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
is measurable as a vector field with respect to the field of Hilbert spaces
\bigl(\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\bigr)_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
, it is also measurable as a field of operators. After possibly passing to a subsequence, we obtain (
[
11
, A 79]
)

\pi(x_{n,N})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\chi_{\Omega_{N}}(\pi)T_{\pi}

strongly for almost all
\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}
. Bounded convergence theorem gives

(4.12)

0=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\Tr\bigl(T_{\pi}^{*}\pi(x_{n,N})a_{\pi}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)=\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Tr\bigl(T_{\pi}^{*}\pi(x_{n,N})a_{\pi}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi).

Since for almost all
\pi
the functional
\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\ni{b}\mapsto\Tr(T_{\pi}^{*}ba_{\pi})\in\mathbb{C}
is normal (and hence strongly continuous on bounded sets), we have

\Tr\bigl(T_{\pi}^{*}\pi(x_{n,N})a_{\pi}\bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\chi_{\Omega_{N}}(\pi)\Tr(T_{\pi}^{*}T_{\pi}{a_{\pi}}).

This convergence together with equation (
4.12
) show that
\Tr(T_{\pi}^{*}T_{\pi}{a_{\pi}})=0
and consequently
T_{\pi}{a_{\pi}}=0
for almost all
\pi\in\Omega_{N}
. Consequently, as
N
was arbitrary and
a_{\pi}
has trivial kernel, we obtain
T=0
which proves surjectivity of
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
. We are left to show point (
4
).

First we show that
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}
is equal to the direct integral
J={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}J_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)
of canonical conjugations on
\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})
. Choose, as usual, orthonormal bases in
\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}
(
\alpha\in\Irr{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}
) in which operators
\uprho_{\alpha}
are diagonal with eigenvalues
\uprho_{\alpha,i}
and let
x
be a matrix element
U^{\alpha}_{i,j}
. Then
\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{t}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha_{j}})^{\mathrm{i}{t}}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}
(
t\in\mathbb{R}
) and hence, by equation (
4.10
), we have

a_{\pi}^{2k}\pi(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=\pi(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})\bigl(\sqrt{\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j}}a_{\pi}\bigr)^{2k},\qquad{k}\in\mathbb{N}.

Consequently

a_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=\pi(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})\bigl(\sqrt{\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j}}a_{\pi}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}=\bigl(\uprho_{\alpha,i}\uprho_{\alpha,j}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})a_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}=\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{t}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})\bigr)a_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}},\qquad{t}\in\mathbb{R}.

By linearity and density we obtain

a_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi(x)=\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{t}(x)\bigr)a_{\pi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}},\qquad{t}\in\mathbb{R}

for all
x\in\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. Take
y\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
. Using the above equation, we obtain

\displaystyle J\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y)

\displaystyle=J{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(y)a_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}a_{\pi}\pi(y^{*})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi\bigl(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{-\mathrm{i}\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}(y^{*})\bigr)a_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}\bigr(\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{-\mathrm{i}\;\!\!/\;\!\!2}}(y^{*})\bigr)=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\nabla^{\scriptscriptstyle{1\>\!\!/\;\!\!2}}_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y^{*})=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y).

Hence the density of
\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}))
in
\Ltwo(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
proves the claim.

To prove that
\bigl(\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},(a_{\pi}^{-1})_{\pi\in\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}},\mu\bigr)
are the left Plancherel objects for
\mathbbl{\Gamma}
, we will use
[
20
, Theorem 3.3 (7)]
. The assumptions of that theorem include that almost all
\pi
are finite dimensional. However, an inspection of the proof shows that a weaker assumption is sufficient – that the operator
D_{\pi}^{-1}
, or in our case
a_{\pi}
, is bounded – which we assume. We need to show that for all
\omega\in\ell^{1}(\mathbbl{\Gamma})

(4.13a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi\bigl((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},

(4.13b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{V}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}\otimes\pi^{\text{\tiny{\rm{c}}}}\bigl((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}},

where
\pi^{\text{\tiny{\rm{c}}}}\colon\C(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\ni{x}\mapsto\pi(R_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(x))^{\top}\in\B(\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}})
(cf.
[
35
, Proposition 10]
). Equality (
4.13a
) follows readily from the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
, so we only need to justify (
4.13b
). We will do this using
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}=J
. Take
y\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})
, a finitely supported
\omega\in\ell^{1}(\mathbbl{\Gamma})
and recall that
\mathrm{V}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}=(J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}\otimes{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}})\widehat{\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}(J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}\otimes{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}})
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\bigl((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{V}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}}\Bigl(\bigl((\omega\circ{R_{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\Bigr)^{*}J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{J_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi\Bigl(\bigl((\omega\circ{R_{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\Bigr)^{*}a_{\pi}\pi(y^{*})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(y)a_{\pi}\pi\Bigl(\bigl((\omega\circ{R_{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(y)a_{\pi}(\pi\circ{R_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}})\bigl((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}\otimes\pi^{\text{\tiny{\rm{c}}}}\bigl((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\pi(y)a_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}\otimes\pi^{\text{\tiny{\rm{c}}}}\bigl((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}(y).

A density argument allows us to conclude that equations (
4.13
) hold. The last element we need to complete the proof is to show that condition (7.3) of
[
20
, Theorem 3.3]
holds, i.e. that
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
transforms
\cZ(\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}))
into diagonalizable operators
\operatorname{Diag}\biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\!\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\biggr)
. This is a consequence of equations (
4.13
). Indeed, from (
4.13
) we obtain

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi),\quad\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})^{\prime}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}\otimes\B(\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\cZ\bigl(\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\bigr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\bigl(\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})\cap\Linf(\widehat{\mathbbl{\Gamma}})^{\prime}\bigr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\B(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\cap{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}}\otimes\B(\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\pi}})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Diag}\Biggl(\,{\int\limits_{\Irr{\mathbbl{\Gamma}}}}^{\!\!\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu}(\pi)\Biggr).\qed

4.2.
General root systems

In this and the subsequent section, we will identify all the invariants for
G_{q}
and its dual (see Remark
2.5
and Theorem
4.12
, Proposition
4.14
, Theorem
4.15
) and give a concrete description for the action of scaling automorphisms (Theorem
4.7
). In particular, we show that in some cases, e.g. for
\operatorname{SU}_{q}(3)
, there exists a non-trivial, inner, scaling automorphism which is not implemented by any group-like unitary. This shows that
\TtauInn(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(3))
is not equal to the invariant
T(\Linf(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(3)),\Delta)
of Vaes (
[
41
, Definition 3.4]
).

We begin our analysis with formulas expressing the action of modular and scaling automorphisms on matrix coefficients of irreducible representations. As in the previous section,
G
is a compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie group and
0<q<1
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Take
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
x\in\Pol(G_{q})
with well defined weights
\lwt(x),\rwt(x)\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
. Then

(1)

\uprho_{\varpi}=\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})\in\B(\mathscr{H}_{\varpi})
,

(2)

\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}_{t}(x)=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)+\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}x
,

(3)

\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}x
.

Proof.

Take
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
with well defined weights. Clearly, in order to prove (
2
) and (
3
) it is enough to consider case
x=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
.

Let
\{f_{z}\,|\,z\in\mathbb{C}\}
be the Woronowicz characters of
G_{q}
(
[
28
, Definition 1.7.1]
). From
[
28
, Proposition 2.4.10]
we know that
f_{1}
is the image of
K_{2\rho}
in
\Pol(G_{q})^{*}
(note that we changed
K_{i}
to
K_{i}^{-1}
to be consistent with the conventions of
[
9
]
), hence we can calculate

{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\uprho_{\varpi}\eta\right\rangle}=f_{1}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)(K_{2\rho})={\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})\eta\right\rangle}.

As
\xi,\eta
with well defined weights span
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
, we obtain (
1
).

Recall that
\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}_{t}(x)=f_{\mathrm{i}{t}}\star{x}\star{f_{\mathrm{i}{t}}}
(
[
28
, Page 30]
), so using
\uprho_{\varpi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}=\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}_{t}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}f_{\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\xi_{i}^{\varpi})\bigr)U^{\varpi}(\xi_{i}^{\varpi},\xi_{j}^{\varpi})f_{\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi_{j}^{\varpi},\eta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}{\left\langle{\xi}\,\vline\,\uprho_{\varpi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\xi_{i}^{\varpi}\right\rangle}\,{\left\langle{\xi_{j}^{\varpi}}\,\vline\,\uprho_{\varpi}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\eta\right\rangle}\,U^{\varpi}(\xi_{i}^{\varpi},\xi_{j}^{\varpi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}{\left\langle{\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\xi}\,\vline\,\xi_{i}^{\varpi}\right\rangle}\,{\left\langle{\xi_{j}^{\varpi}}\,\vline\,\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\eta\right\rangle}\,U^{\varpi}(\xi_{i}^{\varpi},\xi_{j}^{\varpi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{\varpi}}{\left\langle{q^{-\langle 2\rho|\wt(\xi)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}\xi}\,\vline\,\xi_{i}^{\varpi}\right\rangle}\,{\left\langle{\xi_{j}^{\varpi}}\,\vline\,q^{\langle 2\rho|\wt(\eta)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}\eta\right\rangle}\,U^{\varpi}(\xi_{i}^{\varpi},\xi_{j}^{\varpi})

\displaystyle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)+\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}x.

Statement (
3
) can be obtained in an analogous way this time using the formula
\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)=f_{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\star{x}\star{f_{\mathrm{i}{t}}}
.
∎

As in the proof of Proposition
2.3
we let
P
denote the strictly positive, self-adjoint operator on
\Ltwo(G_{q})
which implements the scaling group of
G_{q}
. Using formula for
\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}
from Lemma
4.4
, we are able to describe the action of
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
at the level of the direct integral
{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
. For this we first introduce the following:

Lemma 4.5
.

There is a continuous homomorphism
\mathbb{R}\ni{t}\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}\in{T}
characterized by
\langle\varpi,{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}\rangle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}
for
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
.

Proof.

Clearly
{\boldsymbol{P}}\ni\varpi\mapsto{q^{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}}\in\mathbb{T}
is a continuous homomorphism, hence the existence of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}
follows from Pontriagin duality. To see that
t\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}
is continuous, consider the isomorphisms
T\cong\mathbb{T}^{r}
and
{\boldsymbol{P}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r}
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
the operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\in\B\Bigl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Bigr)
acts as

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}A_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl({|b_{\rho}|}\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Proof.

Take
x=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
for
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
with well defined weights. Using Lemma
4.4
we have

(4.14)

P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}(x)=\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}\Bigl(\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}\bigl(U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)\Bigr)=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}(x).

Meanwhile, Corollary
4.3
implies

(4.15)

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}(x)=\biggl(\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\bigl(1-q^{2\langle\rho|\alpha\rangle}\bigr)\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}}{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Hence, combining (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) gives

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}{P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{t}
be the operator from the claim, i.e.

\mathcal{R}_{t}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}A_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
is unitary, vectors of the form
{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
constitute a linearly dense set in
{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
. Thus it is enough to show that

(4.16)

\mathcal{R}_{t}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

We have
\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(|b_{\rho}|)=\pi_{w_{\circ}}(|b_{\rho}|)
for any
\lambda\in{T}
(equality (
4.4
)). Using equation (
4.1
) and the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
we can calculate the left hand side of (
4.16
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{t}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t},w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}(x)\bigl\langle\lwt(x),\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}\bigr\rangle\pi_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t},w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\lwt(x),{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}\bigr\rangle{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Using

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\nabla_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\otimes\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\bigr)^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

(Corollary
4.3
,
[
21
, Theorem 5.4]
), Lemma
4.4
and the definition of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}
(Lemma
4.5
) we can continue the calculation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{t}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)\rangle 2\mathrm{i}{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\nabla_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}(x)

\displaystyle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)\rangle 2\mathrm{i}{t}}q^{-\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)+\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{h}}_{G_{q}}}(x)

\displaystyle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}\bigl(x|b_{\rho}|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

This shows (
4.16
) and proves the claim.
∎

As a corollary of Proposition
4.6
we obtain an alternative formula for the action of scaling group. Recall (
[
20
, Theorem 3.3]
) that
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}
induces isomorphism between
\Linf(G_{q})
and
{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\B(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
.

Theorem 4.7
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\Linf(G_{q})
with
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}x\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}x_{\lambda}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
we have

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}x_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}.

Proof.

For any
{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\in{\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
we have using the formula for
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
from Proposition
4.6

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}xP^{-\mathrm{i}{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}x_{\lambda}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}A_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{-2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}x_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}A_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{-2t}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

\displaystyle={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}x_{\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}A_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

which proves the claim.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

In
[
21
, Proposition 7.3]
the first named author obtained a similar result in the case of
\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)
. The formula for
P^{\mathrm{i}{t}}
is different – it consists only of rotation, without composition with inner automorphism. This difference is a result of choosing different field of representations
(\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}})_{\lambda\in{T}}
(c.f. the beginning of this section and
[
21
, Proposition 7.1]
).

As a corollary of Theorem
4.7
we obtain equality of invariants
\TtauAInn(G_{q})
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})
.

Proposition 4.9
.

We have
\TtauAInn(G_{q})=\TtauInn(G_{q})
.

Proof.

Take
t\in\TtauAInn(G_{q})
and let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of unitaries in
\Linf(G_{q})
such that
\operatorname{Ad}(u_{n})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}
. Theorem
4.7
shows that in order to deduce
t\in\TtauInn(G_{q})
, it is enough to show that
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}
is the neutral element in
T
. Fix an arbitrary measurable subset
\Omega\subset{T}
and consider central element

x=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\chi_{\Omega}(\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\in\cZ(\Linf(G_{q})).

We have

\displaystyle\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{t}}\chi_{\Omega}(\lambda{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t})\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\chi_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{-2t}\Omega}(\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}.

On the other hand, since
x
is central we have

\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}(x)={\mathrm{w}^{*}\text{-}\!\!}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Ad(u_{n})(x)={\mathrm{w}^{*}\text{-}\!\!}\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}xu_{n}^{*}=x=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\chi_{\Omega}(\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}.

It follows that the symmetric difference
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{-2t}\Omega\div\Omega
is null for all measurable
\Omega\subset{T}
, which clearly implies that
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}={{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{-2t}}^{-1}
is the neutral element in
T
.
∎

In the next theorem (Theorem
4.12
) we show how to calculate the remaining invariants of Section
2
using Lie-theoretic information. We need to start with two lemmas. For what follows, recall that we take the normalization in which
\langle\beta|\beta\rangle=2
for short roots
\beta\in\Phi
.

Lemma 4.10
.

(1)

\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\alpha\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\alpha\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}\bigr\}=2\mathbb{Z}
,

(2)

The set
\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\varpi\in{{\boldsymbol{P}}}\bigr\}
is a nontrivial subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma
4.10
allows us to make the following definition.

Definition 4.11
.

We define
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\in\mathbb{N}
to be the number determined uniquely by

\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}\bigr\}=\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}.

The symbol we use for the positive integer defined above should in principle include a reference to the compact semisimple simply connected Lie group
G
. However for reasons which will become clear below we chose to use notation which refers to the root system
\Phi
of the Lie algebra of
G
(which contains the full information on
G
).

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

Both sets appearing in the statement of Lemma
4.10
are subgroups of
\mathbb{R}
, hence it is enough to show that for
\alpha\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
and
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
we have

(4.17)

\langle 2\rho|\alpha\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z},\qquad\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}

and that there is a root
\alpha\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
satisfying
\langle 2\rho|\alpha\rangle=2
. As
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear span of simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{r}\}
and
{\boldsymbol{P}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear span of the fundamental weights
\{\varpi_{1},\dotsc,\varpi_{r}\}
, in order to show (
4.17
) it is enough to consider
\alpha=\alpha_{i}
and
\varpi=\varpi_{i}
.

Ad (
1
). Write
\rho
as
\rho=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\varpi_{j}
, then

(4.18)

\langle 2\rho|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\varpi_{j}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{j=1}^{r}\delta_{i,j}\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}{2}=\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle.

We claim that
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, as any root system is equal to a product of irreducible ones (
[
14
, Proposition 11.3]
), it is enough to assume that
\Phi
is irreducible. Then for any root
\beta\in\Phi
we have
\langle\beta|\beta\rangle\in\{2,4,6\}
(see
[
14
, Lemma 10.4 C]
and its proof) and consequently
\langle 2\rho|\alpha_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{N}
by (
4.18
). Furthermore, there is
\alpha_{i}\in\Phi
of length
\sqrt{2}
which proves (
1
).

Ad (
2
).
Recall that
\rho
can be expressed also as
\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\beta
. Furthermore, each positive root
\beta\in\Phi^{+}
can be written uniquely as
\beta=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}c(\beta,j)\alpha_{j}
for some
c(\beta,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
(
[
14
, Section 10.1]
). We have

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\langle\beta|\varpi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}c(\beta,j)\langle\alpha_{j}|\varpi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}c(\beta,j)\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}{2}\delta_{i,j}=\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}c(\beta,i)\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}{2}\in\mathbb{N}

again using
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
∎

We can state our main result of this section using number
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
introduced in Definition
4.11
.

Theorem 4.12
.

For any
0<q<1
and compact, simply connected,
semisimple Lie group
G
we have

\Ttau(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}

and

\Mod(\widehat{G_{q}})=\TtauInn(G_{q})=\TtauAInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}.

We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 4.13
.

We have

\bigl\{\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\,\bigr|\bigl.\,x\in\Pol(G_{q})\textnormal{ with well defined weights }\lwt(x),\rwt(x)\in{{\boldsymbol{P}}}\bigr\}={\boldsymbol{Q}}.

Proof.

We will use the result
[
14
, Proposition 21.3]
: weights of
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
are precisely these
\mu\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
which satisfy the following condition: for all
w
in the Weyl group,
\varpi-w\mu
is equal to
0
or can be written as a sum of positive roots.

Take
0\neq{x}=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
, where
\lwt(x)=\wt(\xi),\rwt(x)=\wt(\eta)
. This means that
\wt(\xi),\wt(\eta)
are weights of
\varpi
, hence the result quoted above implies that
\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)=(\varpi-\wt(\eta))-(\varpi-\wt(\xi))
belongs to the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear span of positive roots, i.e. root lattice
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
.

Conversely, take
\mu\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
. By
[
4
, Theorem 20.1]
, there exists
w\in{W}
such that
\varpi=w\mu\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cap{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
. We claim that
\mu
and
0
are weights of
\varpi
. The first statement follows as
\varpi
is a weight of representation
\varpi
and the set of weights of
\varpi
is invariant under the action of Weyl group.

To see that
0
is also a weight of
\varpi
, let us write
\varpi=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
with some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\varpi
is in the positive Weyl chamber, we have
c_{i}\geq 0
. Indeed, let us decompose
\{1,\dotsc,r\}
into
I\cup{J}
in such a way that
c_{i}\geq 0
for
i\in{I}
and
c_{j}<0
for
j\in{J}
. We have

(4.19)

\biggl\langle\sum_{j\in{J}}c_{j}\alpha_{j}\biggr|\biggl.\varpi\biggr\rangle=\sum_{j\in{J}}c_{j}\langle\alpha_{j}|\varpi\rangle\leq 0

because
c_{j}<0
and
\langle\alpha_{j}|\varpi\rangle\geq 0
. On the other hand

(4.20)

\biggl\langle\sum_{j\in{J}}c_{j}\alpha_{j}\biggr|\biggl.\varpi\biggr\rangle=\biggl\|\sum_{j\in{J}}c_{j}\alpha_{j}\biggr\|^{2}+\sum_{j\in{J},i\in{I}}c_{j}c_{i}\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq 0

and both terms are non-negative as
\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\leq 0
by
[
14
, Lemma 10.1]
. It follows from (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) that
\sum\limits_{j\in{J}}c_{j}\alpha_{j}=0
and
\varpi
is a sum of positive roots as claimed. Now
[
14
, Proposition 21.3]
tells us that
0
is a weight of
\varpi
. Consequently, we can take
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}\setminus\{0\}
with
\wt(\xi)=\mu
,
\wt(\eta)=0
and define
x=U^{\varpi}(\xi,\eta)
. Then
\mu=\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.12
.

We begin by calculating
\Ttau(G_{q})
. Take a real number
t
. Using Lemma
4.4
we see that
t\in\Ttau(G_{q})
if and only if
q^{\langle 2\rho|\lwt(x)-\rwt(x)\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}=1
for all
x\in\Pol(G_{q})
with well defined left and right weight. By Lemma
4.13
this is equivalent to
q^{\langle 2\rho|\alpha\rangle\mathrm{i}{t}}=1
for all
\alpha\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
which by Lemma
4.10
reduces to
q^{2k\mathrm{i}{t}}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. This last condition is easily seen to be equivalent to
t\in\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
.

We already proved in Proposition
4.9
that
\TtauAInn(G_{q})=\TtauInn(G_{q})
. Next we calculate
\TtauInn(G_{q})
.

Take
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The expression for
\tau^{G_{q}}_{t}
stated in Theorem
4.7
shows that
t\in\TtauInn(G_{q})
if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}
is the trivial element in
T
. Definition of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2t}
(Lemma
4.5
) shows that this is equivalent to
q^{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle 2\mathrm{i}{t}}=1
for all
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
and a similar argument to the one above shows that this is, in turn, equivalent to
t\in\tfrac{2\pi}{2\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
.

The last step is to calculate
\Mod(\widehat{G_{q}})
. Recall (
[
30
, Theorem 3.3 and Remark on page 402]
, see also
[
21
, Proposition 5.5]
) that
\widehat{\delta}=\bigoplus\limits_{\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}}\uprho_{\varpi}^{2}
. Consequently, by Lemma
4.4
, we have
t\in\Mod(\widehat{G_{q}})
if and only if

\mathds{1}=\uprho_{\varpi}^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}=\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})^{2\mathrm{i}{t}}

for all
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
. Recall also that
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}
is the direct sum of weight spaces
\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu)
and
\pi_{\varpi}(K_{2\rho})\xi=q^{\langle 2\rho|\mu\rangle}\xi
for
\xi\in\mathscr{H}_{\varpi}(\mu)
. Furthermore, any
\mu\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
is a weight of some
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
. Consequently, we have that
t\in\Mod(\widehat{G_{q}})
if and only if
q^{\langle 2\rho|\mu\rangle 2\mathrm{i}{t}}=1
for all
\mu\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
. In the previous paragraph we have seen that this condition is equivalent to
t\in\TtauInn(G_{q})
.
∎

Since
\Linf(G_{q})
is semifinite, Theorem
4.12
describes all the invariants of
G_{q}
and
\widehat{G_{q}}
in terms of one number
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
(see Remark
2.5
). This number is easily computable out of Lie-theoretic data. Nonetheless, one might want to obtain a more precise answer, e.g. what the invariants are for
G_{q}=\operatorname{SU}_{q}(3)
. To provide a more concrete description of
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
(and consequently of the invariants) we will decompose the root system
\Phi
into irreducible parts and describe how to compute
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
out of the corresponding numbers for irreducible components. The computation in the irreducible cases will be performed in the next section.

According to
[
14
, Proposition 11.3]
, we can write
\Phi=\Phi_{1}\cup\dotsm\cup\Phi_{l}
in such a way that for
i\neq{j}
the roots in
\Phi_{i}
and
\Phi_{j}
are pairwise orthogonal and each
\Phi_{k}
is irreducible. Accordingly, the set of simple roots
\Sigma=\{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{r}\}
decomposes into
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup\dotsm\cup\Sigma_{l}
, where
\Sigma_{k}=\Sigma\cap\Phi_{k}
. It follows that the set of fundamental dominant weights for
\Phi
is the union of sets of fundamental dominant weights for
\Phi_{k}
(
1\leq{k}\leq{l}
). Let
\rho_{k}
be the Weyl vector of
\Phi_{k}
. Using formula
\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\alpha
we see that
\rho=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{l}\rho_{k}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\Phi=\Phi_{1}\cup\dotsm\cup\Phi_{l}
be the decomposition of
\Phi
into irreducible parts as described above and let
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
,
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{k}}

(
1\leq{k}\leq{l}
)
be the numbers associated with
\Phi
and
\Phi_{k}

(
1\leq{k}\leq{l}
)
in Definition
4.11
. Then

\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=\operatorname{gcd}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{1}},\dotsc,\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{l}}\bigr).

Proof.

The claim follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{k=1}^{l}\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\varpi\text{ is a fundamental dominant weight of }\Phi_{k}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{k=1}^{l}\bigl\{\langle 2\rho_{k}|\varpi\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\varpi\text{ is a fundamental dominant weight of }\Phi_{k}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{k=1}^{l}\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{k}}\mathbb{Z}

which is equal to
\operatorname{gcd}(\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{1}},\dotsc,\Upsilon_{\!\Phi_{l}})\mathbb{Z}
thanks to the Bézout’s identity.
∎

4.3.
Irreducible cases

In this section we calculate numbers
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
in the case when
\Phi
is an irreducible root system. Thanks to Proposition
4.14
, this knowledge is enough to find
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
for all root systems of compact, simply connected,
semisimple Lie groups. Theorem
4.12
(see also Remark
2.5
) gives us then modular invariants for all quantum groups
G_{q}
and their duals. According to the famous classification result (see
[
4
,
14
]
), if
\Phi
is irreducible then it belongs to one of
4
families of classical Cartan types
A_{n}
(
n\geq 1
),
B_{n}
(
n\geq 2
),
C_{n}
(
n\geq 3
),
D_{n}
(
n\geq 4
) or is one of the
5
exceptional root systems
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
,
F_{4}
,
G_{2}
. Let us now state our main result of this section.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
0<q<1
and let
G
be a compact, simply connected,
simple Lie group with root system
\Phi
. If
\Phi
is of classical type then we have the following possibilities:

•

type
A_{n}
:

G=\operatorname{SU}(n+1)

(
n\geq 1
)
, if
n
is odd then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
, if
n
is even then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
B_{n}
:

G=\operatorname{Spin}(2n+1)

(
n\geq 2
)
, if
n
is odd then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
, if
n
is even then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
C_{n}
:

G=\operatorname{Sp}(2n)

(
n\geq 3
)
, then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
D_{n}
:

G=\operatorname{Spin}(2n)

(
n\geq 4
)
, if
n\in 4\mathbb{N}+\{0,1\}
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
, if
n\in 4\mathbb{N}+\{2,3\}
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
.

In exceptional cases we have the following possibilities:

•

type
E_{6}
:
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
E_{7}
:
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
E_{8}
:
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
F_{4}
:
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
;

•

type
G_{2}
:
then
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and
\TtauInn(G_{q})=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let us start with a general situation, i.e.
G
is a compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie group with root system
\Phi
. Let
A=\bigl[a_{i,j}\bigr]_{i,j=1}^{r}=\Bigl[2\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}\Bigr]_{i,j=1}^{r}
be the Cartan matrix of
\Phi
and
A^{-1}=\bigl[c_{i,j}\bigr]_{i,j=1}^{r}
its inverse. Using the defining relation
\langle\alpha_{i}|\varpi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}{2}
we find that

\alpha_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}2\tfrac{\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{j}\rangle}\varpi_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{j,i}\varpi_{j},

and hence

(4.21)

\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i,k}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}c_{i,k}a_{j,i}\varpi_{j}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\bigl[A^{-1}\bigr]_{i,k}\bigl[A\bigr]_{j,i}\varpi_{j}=\varpi_{k}.

Take
1\leq{i}\leq{r}
. Using (
4.21
) and the fact that
\rho=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\varpi_{j}
we can express
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle
as follows:

(4.22)

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\varpi_{j}|\varpi_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}c_{k,j}\langle\alpha_{k}|\varpi_{i}\rangle=\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{i,j}.

We see that in order to find the number
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}
, we need a description of the inverse Cartan matrix
A^{-1}=\bigl[c_{i,j}\bigr]_{i,j=1}^{r}
. We take this description from
[
45
]
(see also
[
29
, Table 2]
, note that we have to take the transpose of the matrices given in
[
45
]
, as we use a different convention for Cartan matrices). We will now consider each of the cases listed in Theorem
4.15
separately. Note that
r=n
.

Type
A_{n}
(
n\geq 1
)

We have
c_{i,j}=\min\{i,j\}-\tfrac{ij}{n+1}
(
1\leq{i,j}\leq{n}
) and all roots are short (of length squared
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2
). Next, thanks to (
4.22
)

\displaystyle\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\min\{i,j\}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tfrac{ij}{n+1}\biggr)=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{i}j+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}i-\tfrac{i}{n+1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\biggr)

\displaystyle=2\bigl(\tfrac{i(i+1)}{2}+(n-i)i-\tfrac{i}{n+1}\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}\bigr)=(n+1-i)i

for
1\leq{i}\leq{n}
. Consequently
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
in general and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
if
n
is even.

Assume that
n=2k+1
is odd. If
k=0
then
n=1
and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}\rangle=1
. Otherwise, assuming
k>0
, we have

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n}-2k\varpi_{1}+k\varpi_{2}\rangle=(n+1-n)n-2k(n+1-1)+2k(n+1-2)=n-2k=1

and we see that
1\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
. Hence
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
.

If
n=2k
is even then

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}\rangle=2(n+1-1)-2(n+1-2)=2

and
2\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
. Thus
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Type
B_{n}
(
n\geq 2
)

We have
c_{i,j}=\min\{i,j\}
(
1\leq{i}\leq{n}
,
1\leq{j}<n
) and
c_{i,n}=\tfrac{i}{2}
(
1\leq{i}\leq{n}
). Squared lengths of simple roots are
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=4
(
1\leq{i}<n
) and
\langle\alpha_{n}|\alpha_{n}\rangle=2
(notice the use of transposition and rescaling compared to
[
45
,
14
]
). For
1\leq{i}<n
we have

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle=4\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\min\{i,j\}+\tfrac{i}{2}\biggr)=4\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{i}j+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n-1}i+\tfrac{i}{2}\biggr)=4\bigl(\tfrac{i(i+1)}{2}+(n-1-i)i+\tfrac{i}{2}\bigr)=2i(2n-i),

while for
i=n

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n}\rangle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\min\{n,j\}+\tfrac{n}{2}\biggr)=2\bigl(\tfrac{(n-1)n}{2}+\tfrac{n}{2}\bigr)=n^{2}.

Consequently,
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
in general and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
when
n
is even.

Assume that
n=2k
is even. If
k=1
then
n=2
and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}\rangle=2(4-1)-2^{2}=2
, hence
2\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
. Assume now that
k\geq 2
. We have

\displaystyle\langle 2\rho|(6k-4)(2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2})-\varpi_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=(6k-4)\bigl(4(2n-1)-4(2n-2)\bigr)-6(2n-3)

\displaystyle=4(6k-4)-6(4k-3)=2,

so
2\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
, and again
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Consider now the case when
n=2k+1\geq{3}
is odd. We have

\displaystyle\langle 2\rho|-(k^{2}+k)(2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2})+\varpi_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=-(k^{2}+k)\bigl(4(2n-1)-4(2n-2)\bigr)+n^{2}

\displaystyle=-4(k^{2}+k)+(2k+1)^{2}=1,

so that
1\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
and thus
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
.

Type
C_{n}
(
n\geq 3
)

The Cartan matrix of
C_{n}
is the transpose of the Cartan matrix of
B_{n}
. Hence
c_{i,j}=\min\{i,j\}
(
1\leq{i}<n
,
1\leq{j}\leq{n}
) and
c_{n,j}=\tfrac{j}{2}
(
1\leq{j}\leq{n}
). Furthermore, we can construct the root system
C_{n}
as the dual of
B_{n}
and after rescaling we have
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2
(
1\leq{i}<n
) and
\langle\alpha_{n}|\alpha_{n}\rangle=4
. Using (
4.22
) we calculate

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\min\{i,j\}=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{i}j+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}i\biggr)=2\bigl(\tfrac{i(i+1)}{2}+(n-i)i\bigr)=(2n+1-i)i

when
1\leq{i}<n
and

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n}\rangle=4\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tfrac{j}{2}=n(n+1)

when
i=n
. Thus we see that
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq{i}\leq{n}
. Since

\langle 2\rho|-\varpi_{n-1}+\varpi_{n}\rangle=-\bigl(2n+1-(n-1)\bigr)(n-1)+n(n+1)=-(n+2)(n-1)+n(n+1)=2,

we have
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}
and so
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Type
D_{n}
(
n\geq 4
)

The Cartan matrix of
D_{n}
is symmetric, hence so is its inverse. It is given by

c_{i,j}=\begin{cases}i&1\leq{i}\leq{j}\leq{n-2}\\
\tfrac{i}{2}&1\leq{i}\leq{n-2},\>j\in\{n-1,n\}\\
\frac{n-2}{4}&i=n-1,j=n\\
\frac{n}{4}&i=j\in\{n-1,n\}\end{cases}

for
1\leq{i}\leq{j}\leq{n}
. Furthermore
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2
for all
1\leq{i}\leq{n}
. Using equation (
4.22
), we calculate for
1\leq{i}\leq{n-2}

\displaystyle\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{i,j}=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{i}c_{j,i}+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n-2}c_{i,j}+c_{i,n-1}+c_{i,n}\biggr)

\displaystyle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{i}j+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n-2}i+i\biggr)=2\bigl(\tfrac{i(i+1)}{2}+(n-2-i)i+i\bigr)=(2n-i-1)i.

Next

\displaystyle\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}c_{j,n-1}+c_{n-1,n-1}+c_{n-1,n}\biggr)

\displaystyle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\tfrac{j}{2}+\tfrac{n}{4}+\tfrac{n-2}{4}\biggr)=\tfrac{(n-2)(n-1)+2n-2}{2}=\tfrac{n}{2}(n-1)

and

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n}\rangle=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}c_{j,n}+c_{n-1,n}+c_{n,n}\biggr)=2\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\tfrac{j}{2}+\tfrac{n-2}{4}+\tfrac{n}{4}\biggr)=\tfrac{n}{2}(n-1).

It follows that
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
holds for all
n
.

Assume that
n=4k+a
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\in\{0,1\}
. Then
(2n-i-1)i=(8k+2a-i-1)i\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and also
\tfrac{n}{2}(n-1)=\tfrac{(4k+a)(4k+a-1)}{2}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}\rangle=2(2n-2)-2(2n-3)=2

which yields
2\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

On the other hand, assume that
n=4k+a
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\in\{2,3\}
. As before

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}\rangle=2(2n-2)-2(2n-3)=2,

and hence
2\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore,

\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{n}\rangle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}=\tfrac{(4k+a)(4k+a-1)}{2}=8k^{2}+2k(2a-1)+\tfrac{a(a-1)}{2}\in\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}

is odd, since
a\in\{2,3\}
. As
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}
, we conclude that
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}
and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
.

Type
E_{6}

We have
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2
for all
1\leq{i}\leq 6
. Since the Cartan matrix is symmetric, we can take it directly from
[
45
]
(these two properties apply also to
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
). By direct calculation we find generators
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{6}c_{i,j}
(
1\leq{i}\leq{6}
) of
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}
:

\bigl(\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}\rangle,\dotsc,\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{6}\rangle\bigr)=(16,22,30,42,30,16).

Consequently,
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}\subset 2\mathbb{Z}
and since

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{2}-\varpi_{4}\rangle=44-42=2,

we conclude that
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}
giving
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Type
E_{7}

We find that
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}\rangle=34
and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{2}\rangle=49
, therefore

\langle 2\rho|-36\varpi_{1}+25\varpi_{2}\rangle=-36\cdot 34+25\cdot 49=1.

Since also
c_{i,j}\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
for
1\leq{i,j}\leq{7}
, we obtain
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=1
.

Type
E_{8}

Since
\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle=2
and
c_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq{i,j}\leq{8}
, we have
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and consequently
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}\subset 2\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}\rangle=92
and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{3}\rangle=182
. Hence

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{3}\rangle=184-182=2

and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}
, yielding
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Type
F_{4}

After rescaling (if necessary) the squares of lengths of simple roots are given by
\langle\alpha_{1}|\alpha_{1}\rangle=\langle\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{2}\rangle=4
and
\langle\alpha_{3}|\alpha_{3}\rangle=\langle\alpha_{4}|\alpha_{4}\rangle=2
. The Cartan matrix of
F_{4}
is not symmetric, hence we need to take the transpose of the matrix given in
[
45
]
, namely

A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}2&3&2&1\\
3&6&4&2\\
4&8&6&3\\
2&4&3&2\end{bmatrix}

It is clear that
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for
1\leq{i}\leq 4
. Using (
4.22
) we find that
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{3}\rangle=42
and
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{4}\rangle=22
,
and consequently

\langle 2\rho|-\varpi_{3}+2\varpi_{4}\rangle=2.

Thus
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}
and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.

Type
G_{2}

In this case
\langle\alpha_{1}|\alpha_{1}\rangle=2
and
\langle\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{2}\rangle=6
. We again have to take the transpose of the (inverse) Cartan matrix, i.e.
A^{-1}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}2&3\\
1&2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
. We have
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{1}\rangle=10
,
\langle 2\rho|\varpi_{2}\rangle=18
, hence

\langle 2\rho|2\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{2}\rangle=2.

and
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
.
∎

4.4.
The implementing unitaries

Let
0<q<1
and
G
be a compact, simply connected, semisimple Lie group with the corresponding number
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\in\mathbb{N}
introduced in Definition
4.11
(defined by the root system
\Phi
of
G
). Proposition
4.14
and Theorem
4.15
show that we must have
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}\in\{1,2\}
. In this section we will be interested in the case
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
. Theorem
4.12
shows in this case that
\TtauInn(G_{q})
is a proper subgroup of
\Ttau(G_{q})
, more precisely
\tau^{G_{q}}_{\frac{\pi}{2\log{q}}}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Linf(G_{q}))
is inner (i.e. implemented by some unitary in
\Linf(G_{q})
) and non-trivial. The next proposition shows that it cannot however be implemented by a unitary in
\C(G_{q})
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Assume that
\Upsilon_{\!\Phi}=2
and put
{\boldsymbol{t}}=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}
, so that
\tau^{G_{q}}_{{\boldsymbol{t}}}=\Ad(v)
for a unitary element
v\in\Linf(G_{q})
. Then
v\not\in\C(G_{q})
. In particular,
\bigl.\tau^{G_{q}}_{{\boldsymbol{t}}}\bigr|_{\C(G_{q})}
is not inner.

Before we prove Proposition
4.16
, we need to recall a formula expressing operators
\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(b_{\varpi})
in simpler terms. As we mentioned in Section
4.1
, one can calculate that in the case of
G_{q}=\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)
we have
b_{N}=(-\gamma)^{N}
for all
N\in\Irr{\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. In order to keep track of the deformation parameter, we will denote this operator by
\sideset{{}_{\!\!q}}{{}_{N}}{\operatorname{\mathnormal{b}}}\in\Linf(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))
.

Lemma 4.17

(
[
9
]
)
.

Take
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}^{+}
and write
w_{\circ}=s_{i_{1}}\dotsm{s_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}
. Then

(4.23)

\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(b_{\varpi})=\langle\varpi,\lambda\rangle\,\theta_{q_{i_{1}}}\bigl(\sideset{{}_{\!q_{i_{1}}\!\!}}{{}_{\langle\beta_{1}^{\vee}|\varpi\rangle}}{\operatorname{\mathnormal{b}}}\bigr)\otimes\dotsm\otimes\theta_{q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\bigl(\sideset{{}_{\!q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\!\!}}{{}_{\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}^{\vee}|\varpi\rangle}}{\operatorname{\mathnormal{b}}}\bigr)\in\B\bigl(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})^{\otimes\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr)

for all
\lambda\in{T}
, where

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i_{1}}^{\vee},

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=s_{i_{1}}\alpha_{i_{2}}^{\vee},

\displaystyle\;\;\vdots

\displaystyle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=s_{i_{1}}\dotsc{s_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})-1}}}\alpha_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}^{\vee}

are elements of the root system
\Phi
.

In fact, the elements
\beta_{k}=\tfrac{\langle\beta_{k}|\beta_{k}\rangle}{2}\beta_{k}^{\vee}
, (
1\leq{k}\leq\ell(w_{\circ})
) are the positive roots (see
[
4
, Proposition 20.10]
). The formula for
\pi_{w_{\circ}}(b_{\varpi})
is given on
[
9
, Page 203]
and then (
4.1
) gives an easy modification to the general case. One way of deriving equation (
4.23
) is to first establish
S(b_{\varpi})=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{r}a_{k}}q^{2\langle\rho|\varpi\rangle}b_{-w_{\circ}\varpi}
, where
\varpi=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{r}a_{k}\varpi_{k}
. Next one can run an argument analogous to the one leading to
[
31
, Proposition 4.3]
, but for the elements
S(b_{\varpi})^{*}=U^{\varpi}(\eta_{w_{\circ}\varpi},\xi_{\varpi})
. The final step giving (
4.23
) is done by tweaking the obtained expression using the unitary antipode and the fact that
w_{\circ}^{-1}=w_{\circ}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.16
.

Since
\langle\varpi,{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2{\boldsymbol{t}}}\rangle=q^{\langle 2\rho|\varpi\rangle 2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}=1
for
\varpi\in{\boldsymbol{P}}
, we see that
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{2{\boldsymbol{t}}}
is the trivial element in
T
and consequently Theorem
4.7
gives

\tau^{G_{q}}_{{\boldsymbol{t}}}=\Ad\Biggl(\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}\Biggr).

It follows that we can find a unitary
x\in\cZ(\Linf(G_{q}))
for which

(4.24)

v=x\>\!\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}\Biggl({\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}x_{\lambda}\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\Biggr)\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}},

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}x\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}x_{\lambda}\mathds{1}_{\HS(\mathscr{H}_{\lambda})}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
. Define a bounded linear map
\Psi\colon\Linf(G_{q})\to\Linf(T\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})})
via

\Psi(a)\bigl(\lambda,k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr)={\left\langle{e_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\,\vline\,a_{\lambda}{e_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\right\rangle},\qquad\lambda\in{T},\>k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}a\mathcal{Q}_{\text{\tiny{\rm{L}}}}^{*}={\int\limits_{T}}^{\oplus}a_{\lambda}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\overline{\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}}}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}
and
e_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}=e_{k_{1}}\otimes\dotsm\otimes{e_{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}
(
k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
) is the standard orthonormal basis in
\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})^{\otimes\ell(w_{\circ})}
. We claim that

(4.25)

\Psi(v)\not\in\Linf(T)\otimes\Bigl(\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\times\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr)\Bigr).

Using (
4.24
) and (
4.23
) we can calculate

\displaystyle\Psi(v)\bigl(\lambda,k_{1}

\displaystyle,\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr)=x_{\lambda}{\left\langle{e_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\,\vline\,\pi_{w_{\circ}}\bigl(|b_{\rho}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}e_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=x_{\lambda}{\left\langle{e_{k_{1}}}\,\vline\,\theta_{q_{i_{1}}}\bigl(|\sideset{{}_{\!q_{i_{1}}\!\!}}{{}_{\langle\beta_{1}^{\vee}|\rho\rangle}}{\operatorname{\mathnormal{b}}}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}e_{k_{1}}\right\rangle}\dotsm{\left\langle{e_{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\,\vline\,\theta_{q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\bigl(|\sideset{{}_{\!q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\!\!}}{{}_{\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}^{\vee}|\rho\rangle}}{\operatorname{\mathnormal{b}}}|\bigr)^{-2\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{t}}}e_{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=x_{\lambda}q_{i_{1}}^{-2\mathrm{i}{k_{1}}\langle\beta_{1}^{\vee}|\rho\rangle{\boldsymbol{t}}}\dotsm q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}^{-2\mathrm{i}{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}^{\vee}|\rho\rangle{\boldsymbol{t}}}

for almost all
\lambda\in{T}
and
k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Since
q_{i}=q^{\frac{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}{2}}
and
\beta_{i}^{\vee}=\tfrac{2}{\langle\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle}\beta_{i}
for
1\leq{i}\leq{r}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi(v)\bigl(\lambda,k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr)

\displaystyle=x_{\lambda}q^{-2\mathrm{i}{k_{1}}\langle\beta_{1}|\rho\rangle{\boldsymbol{t}}}\dotsm q^{-2\mathrm{i}{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\rho\rangle{\boldsymbol{t}}}

\displaystyle=x_{\lambda}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{k_{1}}\langle\beta_{1}|\rho\rangle}\dotsm\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{k_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\rho\rangle}.

Assume that (
4.25
) does not hold. Then writing
x_{\bullet}
for the function
T\ni\lambda\mapsto{x_{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{C}
, we also have
(x_{\bullet}^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\Psi(v)\in\Linf(T)\otimes(\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})}))
and integrating over the first variable

(4.26)

\Biggl(\int\limits_{T}\cdot\>\!\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\otimes\mathrm{id}\Biggr)\bigl((x_{\bullet}^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\Psi(v)\bigr)\in\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr).

In Lemma
4.10
we proved that
\bigl\{\langle 2\rho|\alpha\rangle\,\bigr|\bigl.\,\alpha\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}\bigr\}=2\mathbb{Z}
. Now, as
\bigl\{\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\bigr\}
is the set of positive roots, we can find
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\langle{n_{1}}\beta_{1}+\dotsm+n_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\rho\rangle=1
. Finally for
p\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\Biggl(\int\limits_{T}\cdot\>\!\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\otimes\mathrm{id}\Biggr)\bigl((x_{\bullet}^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\Psi(v)\bigr)\bigl(p|n_{1}|,\dotsc,p|n_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{p}|n_{1}|\langle\beta_{1}|\rho\rangle}\dotsm\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{p}|n_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\langle\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\rho\rangle}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{p}\langle{n_{1}}\beta_{1}+\dotsm+n_{\ell(w_{\circ})}\beta_{\ell(w_{\circ})}|\rho\rangle}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}{p}}=\begin{cases}1&p\in 2\mathbb{N}\\
-1&p\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{+}+1\end{cases}.

This contradicts (
4.26
) and hence proves validity of (
4.25
).

On the other hand, we will now show that if
a\in\C(G_{q})
, then
\Psi(a)\in\Linf(T)\otimes(\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})}))
. Because of (
4.25
), this will imply
v\not\in\C(G_{q})
.

Since
\Psi
is continuous and
\Linf(T)\otimes(\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})}))
is norm-closed in
\Linf(T\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell(w_{\circ})})
, it is enough to consider
a\in\Pol(G_{q})
. Then

a_{\lambda}=\pi_{\lambda,w_{\circ}}(a)=(\pi_{\lambda}\otimes\theta_{q_{i_{1}}}\circ\rho_{i_{1}}\otimes\dotsm\otimes\theta_{q_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}}}\circ\rho_{i_{\ell(w_{\circ})}})\Delta_{G_{q}}^{(\ell(w_{\circ}))}(a)

for
\lambda\in{T}
(see
[
22
, Lemma 3]
, for the the notation cf. Section
4.1
). Since also
\rho_{i}(\Pol(G_{q}))\subset\Pol(\operatorname{SU}_{q_{i}}(2))
, it is enough to show

\bigl({\left\langle{e_{k}}\,\vline\,\theta_{q}(a)e_{k}\right\rangle}\bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\in\mathbb{C}\mathds{1}+\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{+})

for any
a\in\Pol(\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2))
. By
[
46
, Theorem 1.2]
this claim follows from

{\left\langle{e_{k}}\,\vline\,\theta_{q}\bigl(\alpha^{r}\gamma^{s}{\gamma^{*}}^{t}\bigr)e_{k}\right\rangle}=\delta_{r,0}q^{k(s+t)}={\left\langle{e_{k}}\,\vline\,\theta_{q}\bigl({\alpha^{*}}^{r}\gamma^{s}{\gamma^{*}}^{t}\bigr)e_{k}\right\rangle}

for
k,r,s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

The results established above show that we can find a compact quantum group
G_{q}
and time
{\boldsymbol{t}}=\tfrac{\pi}{2\log{q}}
such that the scaling automorphism
\tau^{G_{q}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
is non-trivial, inner and not implemented by any unitary in
\C(G_{q})
. In particular it is not implemented by a group-like unitary. Using products and bicrossed products one can construct a compact quantum group where these properties hold at a given finite sequence of times, which are independent over
\mathbb{Q}
(c.f. constructions in
[
23
]
).

5.
Other examples

5.1.
The quantum
\operatorname{E}(2)
group

The quantum
\operatorname{E}(2)
(also referred to as the quantum group
\operatorname{E}_{q}(2)
) was originally defined in
[
47
]
. The crucial development of determining the Haar measure of
\mathbb{G}
was accomplished in
[
2
]
. Finally the most comprehensive text dealing with this quantum group is the PhD thesis
[
15
]
. Throughout this section we will write
\mathbb{G}
for
\operatorname{E}_{q}(2)
.

In order to describe this quantum group we first fix a real number
q\in\left]0,1\right[
. Next we consider two operators
v
and
n
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
defined as

v=s^{*}\otimes\mathds{1},\qquad{n}=Q\otimes{s},

where
s
is the shift defined on the standard basis of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
as
e_{k}\mapsto{e_{k+1}}
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
) and
Q
is the positive self-adjoint operator such that
Qe_{k}=q^{k}e_{k}
for all
k
. Noting that
n
is normal with
\operatorname{Sp}(n)=\bigl\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,\bigr|\bigl.\,|z|\in{q}{Z}\bigr\}\cup\{0\}
we define
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
as the closure in
\B(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}))
of the set of sums of the form

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}v^{k}f_{k}(n)

where
f_{k}\in\C_{0}(\operatorname{Sp}(n))
and
f_{k}=0
for almost all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. One easily shows that
v\in\operatorname{M}(\C_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
and that
n
is affiliated with
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
(
[
48
]
). Moreover
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
is generated by
v
and
n
in the sense of
[
49
]
.

The comultiplication
\Delta
describing the quantum group structure of
\mathbb{G}
is defined in
[
47
, Theorem 1.2]
. It is a
morphism
of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras from
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
to
\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\C_{0}(\mathbb{G})
(see
[
48
]
) determined by its values on
v
and
n
which are

\Delta(v)=v\otimes{v},\qquad\Delta(n)=v\otimes{n}\;\dot{+}\;n\otimes{v^{*}}

with
\dot{+}
denoting the closure of the (closable) operator
v\otimes{n}+n\otimes{v^{*}}
.

The Haar measure
{\boldsymbol{h}}
of
\operatorname{E}_{q}(2)
(which is both left and right invariant) was first described in
[
2
]
. The GNS representation corresponding to
{\boldsymbol{h}}
acts on the Hilbert space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and maps
v
to
\mathds{1}\otimes{s^{*}}\otimes{s^{*}}
and
n
to
s\otimes{Q}\otimes\mathds{1}
. Since the unitary
X\colon{e_{k}}\otimes{e_{l}}\mapsto{e_{k}}\otimes{e_{l-k}}
conjugates
s^{*}\otimes{s^{*}}
onto
s^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}
while
X(Q\otimes\mathds{1})X^{*}=Q\otimes\mathds{1}
, we find that
\Linf(\mathbb{G})
is isomorphic to
\Linf(\mathbb{T})\overset{\rule[-1.0pt]{5.0pt}{0.5pt}}{\otimes}\mathsf{N}
, where
\mathsf{N}
is the von Neumann algebra on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
generated by
s
and bounded measurable functions of
Q
. Since the latter is easily seen to contain all compact operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we obtain

(5.1)

\Linf(\mathbb{G})\cong\Linf(\mathbb{T})\overset{\rule[-1.0pt]{5.0pt}{0.5pt}}{\otimes}\B\bigl(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\bigr)

Baaj also proves
\nabla_{\!{\boldsymbol{h}}}=\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes{Q^{2}}
. These two facts immediately show that

\TsigInn(\mathbb{G})=\mathbb{R},\qquad\Tsig(\mathbb{G})=\tfrac{\pi}{\log{q}}\mathbb{Z},

and consequently also
